\displaystyle{\mathcal{I}}_{\rho_{\beta}}(A:C|B)\leq{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathcal{I}}(R),\quad R=d_{A,C},

(S.30)

where
{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathcal{I}}(R)
is a super-polynomially decaying function which depend on
\beta
,
\{A,B,C\}
and fundamental parameters as in Table
1
.

This approximate version of the quantum Markov property also possesses a similar operational meaning to the exact Markov property
[
47
]
, and it plays a critical role in preparing the quantum Gibbs states on a quantum computer
[
146
,
147
,
68
]
.
In one-dimensional cases, the conjecture has been proved at arbitrary
\mathcal{O}(1)
temperatures
[
56
]
, where the decay rate is given by a subexponential form of
e^{e^{-\mathcal{O}(\beta)}\mathcal{O}(R^{1/2})}
.
In dimensions greater than 1, Conjecture
1
is known to be true only in high-temperature regimes, i.e., above a threshold temperature
[
57
]
.
The primary goal of this paper is to prove the conjecture in arbitrary temperature regimes.

S.I.2.1
Relation to entanglement clustering

We also mention a connection between the quantum Markov structure and the bipartite entanglement.
As has been mentioned in Ref.
[
26
]
, the solution of the quantum Markov conjecture immediately includes the exponential clustering of the quantum squashed entanglement.
For an arbitrary quantum state
\rho_{AB}
that is composed of the systems
A
and
B
, the squashed entanglement is defined as follows:

\displaystyle E_{\rm sq}(\rho_{AB}):=\inf_{E}\left\{\frac{1}{2}{\mathcal{I}}_{\rho_{ABE}}(A:B|E)\biggl|{\rm tr}_{E}(\rho_{ABE})=\rho_{AB}\right\},

(S.31)

where
\inf_{E}
is taken over all extensions of
\rho_{AB}
such that
{\rm tr}_{E}(\rho_{ABE})=\rho_{AB}
.
As one of the convenient properties, the squashed entanglement satisfies the faithfulness; that is, it is equal to zero if and only if the quantum state is not entangled
[
88
,
89
]
.

Under the assumption that the inequality (
S.30
) holds, we can derive the exponential clustering for the squashed entanglement in the following way:

\displaystyle E_{\rm sq}(\rho_{\beta,AB})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}{\mathcal{I}}_{\rho_{\beta}}(A:B|C)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathcal{I}}(R).

(S.32)

Now, the ancilla system
E
is chosen as the residual system to
AB
, i.e.,
E\to C=\Lambda\setminus(AB)
.

To utilize the quantum squashed entanglement to upper-bound other entanglement measures, we use the quantity
\delta_{\rho_{AB}}
, which characterizes the minimum distance between the target state and separable (i.e., non-entangled) states:

\displaystyle\delta_{\rho_{AB}}:=\inf_{\sigma_{AB}\in{\rm SEP}(A:B)}\left\|\rho_{AB}-\sigma_{AB}\right\|_{1},

(S.33)

where
{\rm SEP}(A:B)
is a set of quantum states defined on the subsets
A
and
B
that have no entanglement.
One can derive the following upper bound for
\delta_{\rho_{AB}}
in terms of the squashed entanglement as shown in Ref.
[
148
, Corollary 3.13]
(see also Ref.
[
149
]
):

\displaystyle\delta_{\rho_{AB}}\leq 4\min(\mathcal{D}_{A},\mathcal{D}_{B})\sqrt{E_{\rm sq}(\rho_{AB})}\leq 2\min(\mathcal{D}_{A},\mathcal{D}_{B})\sqrt{2{\mathcal{I}}_{\rho_{\beta}}(A:B|E)},

(S.34)

where
\mathcal{D}_{A}
and
\mathcal{D}_{B}
are the dimensions of the Hilbert spaces of
A
and
B
, respectively.
Therefore, if
E_{\rm sq}(\rho_{AB})\ll 1/\mathcal{D}_{AB}
, we can conclude that
\delta_{\rho_{AB}}
is as small as
{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathcal{I}}(R)
from (
S.32
).
In the case where
|AB|
is macroscopically large (i.e.,
|AB|=\mathcal{O}(|\Lambda|)
),

\mathcal{D}_{AB}
is exponentially large with
|\Lambda|
, and hence the inequality (
S.32
) cannot be used to provide an upper bound on other entanglement measures such as the relative entanglement entropy and the EoF.
In the case of one-dimensional systems, we can overcome the problem by employing the prescription introduced in Ref.
[
26
, Theorem 12]
(see also Sec.
S.IX
).

S.I.3
Upper bound on the norm of commutators

In this section, we show several convenient lemmas on the norm of commutators that are often used in our analysis.

Lemma 2
.

Let
O
be an arbitrary operator. Also, for an arbitrary unitary operator
u_{i}
acting on a site
i\in\Lambda
, we assume the following inequality:

\displaystyle\sup_{u_{i}}\left\|[O,u_{i}]\right\|\leq\delta_{i},

(S.35)

where
\sup_{u_{i}}
is taken for the set of all the unitary operators acting on the site
i
.
Then, we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|O-\tilde{{\rm tr}}(O)\right\|\leq\sum_{i\in\Lambda}\delta_{i}.

(S.36)

Also, for an arbitrary operator
O_{X}
which is supported on subset
X\subset\Lambda
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|[O,O_{X}]\right\|\leq 2\left\|O_{X}\right\|\sum_{i\in X}\delta_{i}.

(S.37)

Remark.
The inequality (
S.36
) gives upper bound for the norm of
\left\|O\right\|
when
O
is traceless (i.e.,
{\rm tr}(O)=0
):

\displaystyle\left\|O\right\|\leq\sum_{i\in\Lambda}\delta_{i}\quad\textrm{for}\quad{\rm tr}(O)=0.

(S.38)

Also, when the initial condition is generalized as

\displaystyle\sup_{u_{X}}\left\|[O,u_{X}]\right\|\leq\delta_{X},

(S.39)

one can derive

\displaystyle\left\|[O,O_{X}]\right\|\leq 2\left\|O_{X}\right\|\delta_{X}.

(S.40)

In the above inequality, we upper-bound
\delta_{X}
by
\sum_{i\in\Lambda}\delta_{i}
when we begin with the assumption (
S.35
).

Proof of Lemma
2
.

For the proof, we consider

\displaystyle\tilde{{\rm tr}}_{X}(O)=\frac{\hat{1}_{X}\otimes{\rm tr}_{X}(O)}{{\rm tr}_{X}(\hat{1})},

(S.41)

where we use the definition (
S.13
).
Note that
\tilde{{\rm tr}}_{X}(O)
is supported on
X^{\rm c}
, and hence
[\tilde{{\rm tr}}_{X}(O),O_{X}]=0
.
We now label the sites in
X
by
X=\{1,2,\ldots,|X|\}
without loss of generality.
Using the discussion in Ref.
[
113
]
, we have

\displaystyle\tilde{{\rm tr}}_{X}(O):=\int d\mu(u_{1})d\mu(u_{2})\cdots d\mu(u_{|X|})(u_{1}u_{2}\cdots u_{|X|})^{\dagger}O(u_{1}u_{2}\cdots u_{|X|}),

(S.42)

and hence we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|O-\tilde{{\rm tr}}_{X}(O)\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\int d\mu(u_{1})d\mu(u_{2})\cdots d\mu(u_{|X|})\sum_{i\in X}\left\|[O,u_{i}]\right\|\leq\sum_{i\in X}\sup_{u_{i}}\left\|[O,u_{i}]\right\|\leq\sum_{i\in X}\delta_{i},

(S.43)

where
\mu(u_{i})
is the Haar measure for the unitary operator on the site
i\in\Lambda
.
Therefore, by choosing
X=\Lambda
, we obtain the first main inequality (
S.36
).
Also, by using the inequality of

\displaystyle\left\|[O,O_{X}]\right\|=\left\|[O-\tilde{{\rm tr}}_{X}(O),O_{X}]\right\|\leq 2\left\|O_{X}\right\|\cdot\left\|O-\tilde{{\rm tr}}_{X}(O)\right\|,

(S.44)

we obtain the second main inequality (
S.37
).
This completes the proof of Lemma
2
.
\square

Lemma 3
.

Let
O_{X}
be an arbitrary operator supported on a subset
X\subset\Lambda
.
If
O_{X}(t)
satisfies the Lieb–Robinson bound as

\displaystyle\left\|[O_{X}(t),O_{Y}]\right\|\leq\mathcal{G}(X,Y,t,d_{X,Y})

(S.45)

with
\mathcal{G}(X,Y,t,d_{X,Y})
an appropriate function,
we have

\displaystyle\left\|O_{X}(t)-O_{X[r]}^{(t)}\right\|\leq\mathcal{G}(X,X[r]^{\rm c},t,r+1),\quad O_{X[r]}^{(t)}:=\tilde{{\rm tr}}_{X[r]^{\rm c}}\left[O_{X}(t)\right].

(S.46)

Similarly, we also obtain

\displaystyle\left\|O_{X[r+\delta r]}^{(t)}-O_{X[r]}^{(t)}\right\|\leq\mathcal{G}(X,X[r+\delta r]\setminus X[r],t,r+1),

(S.47)

where the integers
r
and
\delta r
can be arbitrarily chosen.

Proof of Lemma
3
.

The inequality (
S.46
) is immediately proved by using

\displaystyle\tilde{{\rm tr}}_{X[r]^{\rm c}}\left[O_{X}(t)\right]=\int d\mu(U_{X[r]^{\rm c}})U_{X[r]^{\rm c}}^{\dagger}O_{X}(t)U_{X[r]^{\rm c}},

(S.48)

which yields

\displaystyle\left\|O_{X}(t)-\tilde{{\rm tr}}_{X[r]^{\rm c}}\left[O_{X}(t)\right]\right\|\leq\left\|\left[O_{X}(t),U_{X[r]^{\rm c}}\right]\right\|\leq\mathcal{G}(X,X[r]^{\rm c},t,r)

(S.49)

from the inequality (
S.45
) and
d_{X,X[r]^{\rm c}}=r+1
.

We then let
X^{\prime}=X[r+\delta r]\setminus X[r]
.
The second inequality (
S.47
) is derived in the same way as follows:

\displaystyle\left\|O_{X[r+\delta r]}^{(t)}-O_{X[r]}^{(t)}\right\|

\displaystyle=\left\|\tilde{{\rm tr}}_{X[r+\delta r]^{\rm c}}\left[O_{X}(t)\right]-\tilde{{\rm tr}}_{X[r]^{\rm c}}\left[O_{X}(t)\right]\right\|

\displaystyle=\left\|\int d\mu(U_{X[r+\delta r]^{\rm c}})U_{X[r+\delta r]^{\rm c}}^{\dagger}\left(O_{X}(t)-\int d\mu(U_{X^{\prime}})U_{X^{\prime}}^{\dagger}O_{X}(t)U_{X^{\prime}}\right)U_{X[r+\delta r]^{\rm c}}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|O_{X}(t)-\int d\mu(U_{X^{\prime}})U_{X^{\prime}}^{\dagger}O_{X}(t)U_{X^{\prime}}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\int d\mu(U_{X^{\prime}})\left\|\left[O_{X}(t),U_{X^{\prime}}\right]\right\|\leq\mathcal{G}(X,X^{\prime},t,r+1).

(S.50)

This completes the proof of Lemma
3
.
\square

S.I.4
Lieb–Robinson bound and locality analyses

Here, we introduce the Lieb–Robinson bound, which characterizes the quasi-locality of interactions under time evolution.
It plays a central role in our analyses alongside the quantum belief propagation.
One can prove the following statement in general
[
112
,
8
,
150
,
9
]
:

Lemma 4

(Lieb–Robinson bound
[
9
]
)
.

For arbitrary operators
O_{X}
and
O_{Y}
with unit norm and
d_{X,Y}=R
, the norm of the commutator
[O_{X}(t),O_{Y}]
satisfies the following inequality:

\displaystyle\left\|[O_{X}(t),O_{Y}]\right\|\leq C\min(|\partial X|,|\partial Y|)\left(e^{v|t|}-1\right)e^{-\mu R},

(S.51)

where we have adopted
C
and
v
as fundamental parameters in Table
1
, but they can be expressed using the parameters, i.e.,
D
,
\gamma
,
\bar{J}_{0}
and
\mu
.

Remark.

Originally derived inequality in Ref.
[
9
]
is slightly weaker than (
S.51
):

\displaystyle\left\|[O_{X}(t),O_{Y}]\right\|\leq C_{0}\min(|X|,|Y|)\left(e^{v|t|}-1\right)e^{-\mu R}.

(S.52)

Using the inequality (
S.37
) in Lemma
2
, we improve the bound (
S.52
) with
O\to O_{X}(t)
,
O_{X}\to O_{Y}
and
\delta_{i}\to C\left(e^{v|t|}-1\right)e^{-\mu d_{i,X}}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|[O_{X}(t),O_{Y}]\right\|\leq C\left(e^{v|t|}-1\right)\sum_{i\in Y}e^{-\mu d_{i,X}},

(S.53)

and

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in Y}e^{-\mu d_{i,X}}\leq\sum_{s=0}^{\infty}\sum_{i\in(\partial Y)_{-s}}e^{-\mu(d_{X,Y}+s)}\leq|\partial Y|e^{-\mu R}\sum_{s=0}^{\infty}e^{-\mu s}=\frac{|\partial Y|e^{-\mu R}}{1-e^{-\mu}}.

(S.54)

Combining (
S.53
) and (
S.54
) yields the inequality (
S.51
) by letting
C=C_{0}/(1-e^{-\mu})
.

We define
O_{X}(H,t,\tilde{X})
as an approximation of
O_{X}(H,t)
on the subset
\tilde{X}
,

\displaystyle O_{X}(H,t,\tilde{X}):=\frac{1}{{\rm tr}_{\tilde{X}^{\rm c}}(\hat{1})}{\rm tr}_{\tilde{X}^{\rm c}}\left[O_{X}(H,t)\right]\otimes\hat{1}_{\tilde{X}^{\rm c}},

(S.55)

where
{\rm tr}_{\tilde{X}^{\rm c}}(\cdots)
is the partial trace with respect to the subset
\tilde{X}^{\rm c}
.
We then obtain from Lemma
3

\displaystyle\left\|O_{X}(H,t)-O_{X}(H,t,X[r])\right\|\leq C\|O_{X}\|\cdot|\partial X|\left(e^{v|t|}-1\right)e^{-\mu r},

(S.56)

where the notation
X[r]
has been defined in Eq. (
S.4
).

Throughout the proof, we consider the Lieb–Robinson bound for short-range interacting (or with exponentially decaying interactions) systems, but the Lieb–Robinson bound holds for more general classes of Hamiltonians with power-law decaying (i.e., long-range) interactions
[
151
,
152
,
153
,
154
]
.
As long as the Lieb–Robinson bound holds, we expect that our main results will be generalized to the long-range interacting systems.

S.I.5
Commutator analysis of different quasi-local operators

In this section, we address the following question.
Consider two quasi-local operators,
O_{1}
and
O_{2}
.
A fundamental problem is to estimate the degree of quasi-locality of their commutator,
\left[O_{1},O_{2}\right]
.
Such an analysis of locality for distinct quasi-local operators plays a central role in our study.
Related techniques have also been developed in the context of the Lieb–Robinson bound
[
153
,
182
]
.
The following lemma characterizes the commutator norm of
\int_{-\infty}^{-\infty}f(t)\left\|\left[O,u_{i}(t)\right]\right\|dt
(see also Fig.
8
), where
u_{i}(t)
is characterized by the time evolution for a unitary operator at the site
i\in\Lambda
and
f(t)
is a filter function.
This also characterizes the quasi-locality of the operator
\int_{-\infty}^{-\infty}f(t)O(-t)dt
.

Figure 8:
Schematic picture of the setup of Lemma
5
.
We consider two quasi-local operators; the former one is
O
that satisfies the quasi-locality described by (
S.57
), while the latter one is given by
u_{i}(t)
[or
u_{i}(\lambda t)
with
0\leq\lambda\leq 1
].
The commutator between
O
and
\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f(t)u_{i}(\lambda t)dt
is upper-bounded as in (
S.58
).
In the proof, we decompose the operator
u_{i}(\lambda t)
as in Eq. (
S.61
) and take the commutator with each of the decomposed terms as in (
S.63
).

Lemma 5
.

Let
O
be a quasi-local operator around a subset
\mathfrak{L}
in the sense that

\displaystyle\left\|[O,u_{i}]\right\|\leq{\mathcal{F}}(\ell),\quad\ell=d_{i,\mathfrak{L}}

(S.57)

for an arbitrary unitary operator on a site
i
, where
{\mathcal{F}}(\ell)
is a monotonically decaying function. Then, under the Lieb–Robinson bound in short-range interacting systems, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f(t)\left\|[O,u_{i}(\lambda t)]\right\|dt\leq 2f_{1}\mathcal{F}(\ell)+2\gamma(\Delta\ell)^{D}\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}s^{D}\mathcal{F}(\ell-s\Delta\ell)\left[Cf_{2}ve^{-\mu s\Delta\ell/2}+f_{t_{0}}(s)\right]

(S.58)

for
0\leq\lambda\leq 1
, where we define

\displaystyle f_{t_{0}}(s):=\int_{|t|\geq t_{0}}f(t)dt,\quad f_{1}:=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f(t)dt,\quad f_{2}:=\int_{|t|\leq t_{0}}|t|f(t)dt\leq\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|t|f(t)dt,

(S.59)

where
t_{0}=\max(0,\mu s\Delta\ell/(2v)-\mu(\Delta\ell-1)/v)
, and
f(t)
is an arbitrary positive function.
In particular, by taking
\Delta\ell=1
, the inequality (
S.58
) reduces to

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f(t)\left\|[O,u_{i}(\lambda t)]\right\|dt\leq 2f_{1}\mathcal{F}(\ell)+2\gamma\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}s^{D}\mathcal{F}(\ell-s)\left[Cf_{2}ve^{-\mu s/2}+f_{t_{0}}(s)\right],

(S.60)

where
t_{0}=\mu s/(2v)
.

Remark.

The lemma is utilized in Propositions
23
and
25
for example, where we let the filter function as the function
g_{\beta}(t)
in the quantum belief propagation (
S.91
), which plays an essential role in Lemma
18
for connection of exponential operators.

Proof of Lemma
5
.

We first decompose

\displaystyle u_{i}(\lambda t)=u_{i}^{(t)}+\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}\left(u_{i[\ell_{s}]}^{(t)}-u_{i[\ell_{s-1}]}^{(t)}\right),\quad u_{i[\ell_{s}]}^{(t)}:=\tilde{{\rm tr}}_{i[\ell_{s}]^{\rm c}}\left[u_{i}(\lambda t)\right],

(S.61)

where we use
u_{i[\infty]}^{(t)}=u_{i}(\lambda t)
and define
\ell_{s}
as

\displaystyle\ell_{s}:=s\Delta\ell.

(S.62)

Using the inequality (
S.37
) in Lemma
2
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\left[O,u_{i[\ell_{s}]}^{(t)}-u_{i[\ell_{s-1}]}^{(t)}\right]\right\|

\displaystyle\leq 2\left\|u_{i[\ell_{s}]}^{(t)}-u_{i[\ell_{s-1}]}^{(t)}\right\|\sum_{i^{\prime}\in i[\ell_{s}]}\sup_{u_{i^{\prime}}}\left\|[O,u_{i^{\prime}}]\right\|

\displaystyle\leq 2\left\|u_{i[\ell_{s}]}^{(t)}-u_{i[\ell_{s-1}]}^{(t)}\right\|\cdot|i[\ell_{s}]|\mathcal{F}(\ell-\ell_{s})

\displaystyle\leq 2\gamma\ell_{s}^{D}\mathcal{F}(\ell-\ell_{s})\left\|u_{i[\ell_{s}]}^{(t)}-u_{i[\ell_{s-1}]}^{(t)}\right\|,

(S.63)

where we use
d_{i^{\prime},\mathfrak{L}}\geq d_{i,\mathfrak{L}}-\ell_{s}=\ell-\ell_{s}
for
i^{\prime}\in i[\ell_{s}]
and
d_{i,\mathfrak{L}}=\ell
.
In the same way, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\left[O,u_{i}^{(t)}\right]\right\|

\displaystyle\leq 2\left\|u_{i}^{(t)}\right\|\mathcal{F}(\ell)=2\mathcal{F}(\ell),

(S.64)

where we use
\left\|\tilde{{\rm tr}}_{i^{\rm c}}\left[u_{i}(\lambda t)\right]\right\|\leq\left\|u_{i}(\lambda t)\right\|=1
.

Moreover, by combining the Lieb–Robinson bound and the inequality (
S.47
) in Lemma
3
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|u_{i[\ell_{s}]}^{(t)}-u_{i[\ell_{s-1}]}^{(t)}\right\|\leq\min\left[1,C\left(e^{v|\lambda t|}-1\right)e^{-\mu(\ell_{s-1}+1)}\right]\leq\min\left[1,C\left(e^{v|t|}-1\right)e^{-\mu(\ell_{s}-\Delta\ell+1)}\right],

(S.65)

where we use
0\leq\lambda\leq 1
.
Combining the inequalities (
S.63
) and (
S.65
), we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\left[O,u_{i[\ell_{s}]}^{(t)}-u_{i[\ell_{s-1}]}^{(t)}\right]\right\|

\displaystyle\leq 2\gamma\ell_{s}^{D}\mathcal{F}(\ell-\ell_{s})\min\left[1,C\left(e^{v|t|}-1\right)e^{-\mu(\ell_{s}-\Delta\ell+1)}\right].

(S.66)

Therefore, we can derive for
\left\|\left[O,u_{i}(t)\right]\right\|

\displaystyle\left\|\left[O,u_{i}(\lambda t)\right]\right\|\leq 2\mathcal{F}(\ell)+2\gamma\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}\ell_{s}^{D}\mathcal{F}(\ell-\ell_{s})\min\left[1,C\left(e^{v|t|}-1\right)e^{-\mu(\ell_{s}-\Delta\ell+1)}\right],

(S.67)

whose integral then reads

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f(t)\left\|\left[O,u_{i}(\lambda t)\right]\right\|dt

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathcal{F}(\ell)\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f(t)dt+2\gamma\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}\ell_{s}^{D}\mathcal{F}(\ell-\ell_{s})\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f(t)\min\left[1,C\left(e^{v|t|}-1\right)e^{-\mu(\ell_{s}-\Delta\ell+1)}\right]dt.

(S.68)

Let us set
t_{0}=\max\left(0,\mu\ell_{s}/(2v)-\mu(\Delta\ell-1)/v\right)
and define
f_{t_{0}}(s)
as in Eq. (
S.59
).
We then obtain

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f(t)\min\left[1,C\left(e^{v|t|}-1\right)e^{-\mu(\ell_{s}-\Delta\ell+1)}\right]dt

\displaystyle\leq\int_{|t|\leq t_{0}}f(t)C\left(e^{v|t|}-1\right)e^{-\mu(\ell_{s}-\Delta\ell+1)}dt+\int_{|t|\geq t_{0}}f(t)dt

\displaystyle\leq Cve^{-\mu\ell_{s}/2}\int_{|t|\leq t_{0}}|t|f(t)dt+f_{t_{0}}(s)

\displaystyle\leq Cve^{-\mu\ell_{s}/2}\int_{|t|\leq t_{0}}|t|f(t)dt+f_{t_{0}}(s),

(S.69)

where we use
e^{v|t|}-1\leq v|t|e^{v|t|}\leq v|t|e^{\mu\ell_{s}/2+\mu(-\Delta\ell+1)}
for
|t|\leq t_{0}
.

By applying the inequality (
S.69
) to Eq. (
S.68
), we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f(t)\left\|\left[O,u_{i}(t)\right]\right\|dt

\displaystyle\leq 2f_{1}\mathcal{F}(\ell)+2Cf_{2}v\gamma\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}\ell_{s}^{D}\mathcal{F}(\ell-\ell_{s})e^{-\mu\ell_{s}/2}+2\gamma\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}\ell_{s}^{D}\mathcal{F}(\ell-\ell_{s})f_{t_{0}}(s),

(S.70)

which reduces to the desired inequality (
S.58
) by letting
\ell_{s}=s\Delta\ell
.
This completes the proof.
\square

[ End of Proof of Lemma
5
]

We can also prove a similar statement using the same proof technique:

Corollary 6
.

Under the same setup as in Lemma
5
, we consider

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f(t)\int_{0}^{t}\left\|[O,u_{i}(t_{1})]\right\|dt_{1}dt.

(S.71)

Then, the same inequality as (
S.58
) holds for (
S.71
) by replacing
f(t)
by
|t|f(t)
, where the definitions of
f_{t_{0}}(s)
,
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
are replaced correspondingly.

Proof of Corollary
6
.

We start from the inequality (
S.67
).
By integrating it from
t_{1}=0
to
t_{1}=t
in (
S.67
), we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\left\|\left[O,u_{i}(t_{1})\right]\right\|dt_{1}\leq 2|t|\mathcal{F}(\ell)+2\gamma\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}\ell_{s}^{D}\mathcal{F}(\ell-\ell_{s})\min\left[|t|,C\left(\frac{e^{v|t|}-1}{v}-|t|\right)e^{-\mu(\ell_{s}-\Delta\ell+1)}\right],

(S.72)

Then, the inequality (
S.69
) is replaced by

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f(t)\min\left[|t|,C\left(\frac{e^{v|t|}-1}{v}-|t|\right)e^{-\mu(\ell_{s}-\Delta\ell+1)}\right]dt

\displaystyle\leq\int_{|t|\leq t_{0}}f(t)C\left(\frac{e^{v|t|}-1}{v}-|t|\right)e^{-\mu(\ell_{s}-\Delta\ell+1)}dt+\int_{|t|\geq t_{0}}|t|f(t)dt.

(S.73)

Using
e^{v|t|}-1\leq v|t|e^{v|t|}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{e^{v|t|}-1}{v}-|t|\leq|t|\left(e^{v|t|}-1\right)\leq v|t|^{2}.

(S.74)

By integrating (
S.72
) by multiplying
f(t)
and applying the inequalities (
S.73
) and (
S.74
),
we obtain the similar inequality to (
S.58
) with
f(t)
replaced by
|t|f(t)
.
This completes the proof of Corollary
6
.
\square

S.I.6
1D version

From the inequality (
S.58
) in Lemma
5
, we can roughly estimate

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f(t)\left\|[O,u_{i}(\lambda t)]\right\|dt\sim\Theta(\xi^{D})\mathcal{F}(\ell),

(S.75)

if
f(t)
decays exponentially with
t/\xi
, i.e.,
f(t)\sim e^{-\mathcal{O}(t/\xi)}
, where we choose
\Delta\ell
such that
\Delta\ell\propto\xi
.
Using the dependence, we cannot obtain the best upper bound for the conditional mutual information as in Theorem
5
.
We will also show the point in Sec.
S.VII
.
We, in the following, derive the improved statement by adopting a slightly different ansatz for the quasi-locality of the operator [see (
S.76
)]:

Lemma 7
.

Let
O
be a quasi-local operator around a subset
\mathfrak{L}
in the sense that

\displaystyle\left\|[O,u_{X}]\right\|\leq{\mathcal{F}}(\ell),\quad d_{X,\mathfrak{L}}=\ell,

(S.76)

where the unitary operator
u_{X}
is arbitrarily chosen, and
{\mathcal{F}}(\ell)
is a monotonically decaying function. Then, under the Lieb–Robinson bound in short-range interacting systems, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f(t)\left\|[O,u_{X}(t)]\right\|dt\leq 2f_{1}\mathcal{F}(\ell)+2\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{F}(\ell-s\Delta\ell)\left[C|\partial(\mathfrak{L}[\ell])|f_{2}ve^{-\mu s\Delta\ell/2}+f_{t_{0}}(s)\right]

(S.77)

for an arbitrary choice of
\Delta\ell
,
where we adopt the same definitions for
f_{1}
,
f_{2}
and
f_{t_{0}}(s)
as in Eq. (
S.59
) with
t_{0}=\max(0,\mu s\Delta\ell/(2v)-\mu(\Delta\ell-1)/v)
.

Remark.
We note that the condition (
S.76
) implies the local approximation of

\displaystyle\left\|O-\tilde{{\rm tr}}_{\mathfrak{L}[\ell-1]^{\rm c}}(O)\right\|\leq{\mathcal{F}}(\ell),

(S.78)

where we use the upper bound (
S.46
).
The term
|\partial(\mathfrak{L}[\ell])|
scales as
\ell^{D-1}
concerning
\ell
, and hence, in dimensions larger than 1, we have an upper bound like
\mathcal{O}(\ell^{D-1})\mathcal{F}(\ell)
in (
S.77
), while the inequality (
S.58
) gives an upper bound of
\mathcal{O}(\ell^{0})\mathcal{F}(\ell)
.
Hence, the upper bound (
S.77
) yields a rather weaker bound in high-dimensional systems.
However, in the 1D case, this lemma leads to a qualitatively better estimation of the correlation length of the conditional mutual information (see Sec.
S.VII
).

Proof of Lemma
7
.

We choose
X=\mathfrak{L}[\ell-1]^{\rm c}
since any unitary operator
u_{X}
with
d_{X,\mathfrak{L}}=\ell
can be expressed in the form of
u_{\mathfrak{L}[\ell-1]^{\rm c}}
.
For simplicity of notation, we here introduce
X_{s}
as

\displaystyle X_{s}=\mathfrak{L}[\ell-1-\ell_{s}]^{\rm c},\quad\ell_{s}=s\Delta\ell,

(S.79)

where
X=\mathfrak{L}[\ell-1]^{\rm c}=X_{0}
. We then decompose

\displaystyle u_{X_{0}}(t)=u_{X_{0}}^{(t)}+\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}\left(u_{X_{s}}^{(t)}-u_{X_{s-1}}^{(t)}\right),\quad u_{X_{s}}^{(t)}:=\tilde{{\rm tr}}_{X_{s}^{\rm c}}\left[u_{X}(t)\right].

(S.80)

Using the initial condition (
S.76
), we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\left[O,u_{X_{s}}^{(t)}-u_{X_{s-1}}^{(t)}\right]\right\|

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathcal{F}(\ell-\ell_{s})\left\|u_{X_{s}}^{(t)}-u_{X_{s-1}}^{(t)}\right\|,

(S.81)

where we use the inequality (
S.40
) with
O_{X}\to u_{X_{s}}^{(t)}-u_{X_{s-1}}^{(t)}
and
d_{X_{s},\mathfrak{L}}=\ell-\ell_{s}
.
Moreover, by applying the Lieb–Robinson bound to the inequality (
S.47
) in Lemma
3
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|u_{X_{s}}^{(t)}-u_{X_{s-1}}^{(t)}\right\|\leq\min\left[1,C|\partial X|\left(e^{v|t|}-1\right)e^{-\mu(\ell_{s}-\Delta\ell+1)}\right],

(S.82)

where we use
\ell_{s-1}=\ell_{s}-\Delta\ell
.
Combining the inequalities (
S.81
) and (
S.82
), we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\left[O,u_{X_{s}}^{(t)}-u_{X_{s-1}}^{(t)}\right]\right\|

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathcal{F}(\ell-\ell_{s})\min\left[1,C|\partial(\mathfrak{L}[\ell])|\left(e^{v|t|}-1\right)e^{-\mu(\ell_{s}-\Delta\ell+1)}\right],

(S.83)

where we use the definition of
X=\mathfrak{L}[\ell-1]^{\rm c}
, which gives
\partial X=\partial(\mathfrak{L}[\ell])
.
We then rely on similar analyses to the derivation of (
S.69
).
We set
t_{0}=\max\left(0,\mu\ell_{s}/(2v)-\mu(\Delta\ell-1)/v\right)
and define
f_{t_{0}}(s)
as in Eq. (
S.59
).
Under the choice, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f(t)\min\left[1,C\left(e^{v|t|}-1\right)e^{-\mu(\ell_{s}-\Delta\ell+1)}\right]dt

\displaystyle\leq\int_{|t|\leq t_{0}}f(t)C\left(e^{v|t|}-1\right)e^{-\mu\ell_{s}+\mu(\Delta\ell-1)}dt+\int_{|t|\geq t_{0}}f(t)dt

\displaystyle\leq Cve^{-\mu\ell_{s}/2}\int_{|t|\leq t_{0}}|t|f(t)dt+f_{t_{0}}(s)

\displaystyle\leq Cve^{-\mu\ell_{s}/2}\int_{|t|\leq t_{0}}|t|f(t)dt+f_{t_{0}}(s),

(S.84)

where we use
e^{v|t|}-1\leq v|t|e^{v|t|}\leq v|t|e^{\mu\ell_{s}/2-\mu(\Delta\ell-1)}
for
t\leq t_{0}
.
By combining the inequalities (
S.83
) and (
S.84
), we reach the inequality of

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f(t)\left\|\left[O,u_{X}(t)\right]\right\|dt\leq 2f_{1}\mathcal{F}(\ell)+2\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{F}(\ell-s\Delta\ell)\left[C|\partial(\mathfrak{L}[\ell])|f_{2}ve^{-\mu s\Delta\ell/2}+f_{t_{0}}(s)\right].

(S.85)

This completes the proof of Lemma
7
.
\square

S.II
Quantum Belief propagation

Here, we introduce the quantum belief propagation
[
106
,
107
,
56
]
:

Lemma 8
.

For arbitrary operators
{\mathcal{A}}
and
{\mathcal{B}}
, we consider the following decomposition:

\displaystyle e^{\beta({\mathcal{A}}+{\mathcal{B}})}=\Phi_{{\mathcal{B}}}e^{\beta{\mathcal{A}}}\Phi_{{\mathcal{B}}}^{\dagger}

(S.86)

for a fixed
\beta
. Then, the quantum belief propagation provides the explicit form of
\Phi_{{\mathcal{B}}}
as follows:

\displaystyle\Phi_{{\mathcal{B}}}:=\mathcal{T}e^{\int_{0}^{1}\phi_{{\mathcal{B}},\tau}d\tau},

\displaystyle\phi_{{\mathcal{B}},\tau}:=\frac{\beta}{2}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f_{\beta}(t){\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{A}}_{\tau},t)dt,

(S.87)

where
\mathcal{T}
is the time ordering operator,
{\mathcal{A}}_{\tau}={\mathcal{A}}+\tau{\mathcal{B}}
, and
f_{\beta}(t)
is defined as

\displaystyle f_{\beta}(t)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\tilde{f}_{\beta}(\omega)e^{-i\omega t}d\omega,\quad\tilde{f}(\omega):=\frac{\tanh(\beta\omega/2)}{\beta\omega/2}.

(S.88)

Note that
\phi_{{\mathcal{B}},\tau}
and
\Phi_{{\mathcal{B}}}
are Hermitian operators.

Remark.

The explicit form of
f_{\beta}(t)
can be calculated as
[
65
]

\displaystyle f_{\beta}(t)=\frac{2}{\beta\pi}\log\left(\frac{e^{\pi|t|/\beta}+1}{e^{\pi|t|/\beta}-1}\right)\leq\frac{2}{\beta\pi}\cdot\frac{2}{e^{\pi|t|/\beta}-1}.

(S.89)

Note that
f_{\beta}(t)
is a positive real function that decays exponentially with
t
.
Also, the form of (
S.87
) is different from the original one by Hastings
[
106
]
.
In fact, we will also utilize an equivalent but different version by Hastings, which has been defined as follows:

\displaystyle e^{\beta({\mathcal{A}}+{\mathcal{B}})}=\hat{\Phi}_{{\mathcal{B}}}e^{\beta{\mathcal{A}}}\hat{\Phi}_{{\mathcal{B}}}^{\dagger},

(S.90)

where the quantum belief propagation operator has the form of

\displaystyle\hat{\Phi}_{{\mathcal{B}}}:=\mathcal{T}e^{\int_{0}^{1}\hat{\phi}_{{\mathcal{B}},\tau}d\tau},

\displaystyle\hat{\phi}_{{\mathcal{B}},\tau}:=\frac{\beta{\mathcal{B}}}{2}+i\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}g_{\beta}(t){\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{A}}_{\tau},t)dt,

(S.91)

where
g_{\beta}(t)
is defined as

\displaystyle g_{\beta}(t):=-\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}{\rm sign}(t)e^{-2\pi m|t|/\beta}=-{\rm sign}(t)\frac{e^{-2\pi|t|/\beta}}{1-e^{-2\pi|t|/\beta}}.

(S.92)

The two forms (
S.87
) and (
S.91
) for the belief propagation are equivalent.
One advantage of the form (
S.87
) is that the integral of
f_{\beta}(t)
is convergent, i.e.,

\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f_{\beta}(t)dt=\tilde{f}_{\beta}(\omega=0)=1
, while the integral of
|g_{\beta}(t)|
is not convergent
because of
|g_{\beta}(t)|\propto 1/|t|
for
|t|\ll 1
.
However, the form (
S.90
) from the belief propagation plays a critical role in estimating the quasi-locality due to the connection of exponential operators as in Lemma
18
below.

S.II.0.1
Proof of Lemma
8

We give a simpler proof for the belief propagation based on the Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff (BCH) formula.
Our purpose is to derive the Hermitian operator
\phi
such that

\displaystyle e^{\epsilon\phi}e^{\beta{\mathcal{A}}}e^{\epsilon\phi}=e^{\beta({\mathcal{A}}+\epsilon{\mathcal{B}})}

(S.93)

for infinitesimally small
\epsilon
.
From the BCH formula (see Ref.
[
155
, Eq. (2.7)]
for example), we have in general

\displaystyle e^{\epsilon\phi}e^{\beta{\mathcal{A}}}e^{\epsilon\phi^{\dagger}}=\exp\left[\beta{\mathcal{A}}+\epsilon\left(\frac{\beta{\rm ad}_{{\mathcal{A}}}}{e^{\beta{\rm ad}_{{\mathcal{A}}}}-1}\phi+{\rm h.c.}\right)+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2})\right].

(S.94)

Hence, for
\phi=\phi^{\dagger}
, we have

\displaystyle\beta{\mathcal{B}}=\frac{\beta{\rm ad}_{{\mathcal{A}}}}{e^{\beta{\rm ad}_{{\mathcal{A}}}}-1}\phi+{\rm h.c.}

(S.95)

up to an error of
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2})
.
We define
{\mathcal{B}}_{\omega}=\sum_{i,j}\bra{a_{i}}{\mathcal{B}}\ket{a_{j}}\delta(a_{i}-a_{j}-\omega)\ket{a_{i}}\bra{a_{j}}
and

\phi_{\omega}=\sum_{i,j}\bra{a_{i}}\phi\ket{a_{j}}\delta(a_{i}-a_{j}-\omega)\ket{a_{i}}\bra{a_{j}}
with
\{\ket{a_{i}}\}
the eigenstates of
{\mathcal{A}}
.
We notice that

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}_{\omega}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{A}},t)e^{-i\omega t}dt,\quad{\mathcal{B}}=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}{\mathcal{B}}_{\omega}d\omega.

(S.96)

Then, we obtain
{\rm ad}_{{\mathcal{A}}}(\phi_{\omega})=\omega\phi_{\omega}
(
[{\rm ad}_{{\mathcal{A}}}(\phi_{\omega})]^{\dagger}=\omega\phi_{-\omega}
),
and hence the equation (
S.95
) yields

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}_{\omega}=\left(\frac{\omega}{e^{\beta\omega}-1}+\frac{\omega}{1-e^{-\beta\omega}}\right)\phi_{\omega}=\frac{\omega}{\tanh(\beta\omega/2)}\phi_{\omega}\to\phi_{\omega}=\frac{\tanh(\beta\omega/2)}{\omega}{\mathcal{B}}_{\omega}.

(S.97)

Therefore, we have from
\phi=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\phi_{\omega}d\omega
[see Eq. (
S.96
)]

\displaystyle\phi=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\tanh(\beta\omega/2)}{\omega}{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{A}},t)e^{-i\omega t}dtd\omega=\frac{\beta}{2}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f_{\beta}(t){\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{A}},t)dt,

(S.98)

where we used

\displaystyle f_{\beta}(t)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\tanh(\beta\omega/2)}{\beta\omega/2}e^{-i\omega t}d\omega=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\tilde{f}_{\beta}(\omega)e^{-i\omega t}d\omega.

(S.99)

By repeating the process of Eq. (
S.93
) iteratively, we can prove Lemma
8
.

In deriving Eq. (
S.90
), we start from a more general form as

\displaystyle e^{\epsilon(\phi_{1}+i\phi_{2})}e^{\beta{\mathcal{A}}}e^{\epsilon(\phi_{1}-i\phi_{2})}=\exp\left[\beta{\mathcal{A}}+\epsilon\left(\frac{\beta{\rm ad}_{{\mathcal{A}}}}{e^{\beta{\rm ad}_{{\mathcal{A}}}}-1}\phi_{1}+{\rm h.c.}\right)+i\epsilon\left(\frac{\beta{\rm ad}_{{\mathcal{A}}}}{e^{\beta{\rm ad}_{{\mathcal{A}}}}-1}\phi_{2}-{\rm h.c.}\right)+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2})\right].

(S.100)

We then need to choose
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}
such that

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\omega}{e^{\beta\omega}-1}+\frac{\omega}{1-e^{-\beta\omega}}\right)\phi_{1,\omega}+i\left(\frac{\omega}{e^{\beta\omega}-1}-\frac{\omega}{1-e^{-\beta\omega}}\right)\phi_{2,\omega}=\frac{\omega}{\tanh(\beta\omega/2)}\phi_{1,\omega}-i\omega\phi_{2,\omega}={\mathcal{B}}_{\omega}.

(S.101)

Then, choosing
\phi_{1,\omega}=\beta{\mathcal{B}}_{\omega}/2
gives
\phi_{2,\omega}
as

\displaystyle\phi_{2,\omega}=\frac{1}{i\omega}\left(\frac{\beta\omega/2}{\tanh(\beta\omega/2)}-1\right){\mathcal{B}}_{\omega}.

(S.102)

Thus, the integral of
\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left(\phi_{1,\omega}\pm i\phi_{2,\omega}\right)d\omega
gives

\displaystyle\phi_{1}\pm i\phi_{2}=\frac{\beta{\mathcal{B}}}{2}\pm\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\omega}\left(\frac{\beta\omega/2}{\tanh(\beta\omega/2)}-1\right){\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{A}},t)e^{-i\omega t}dtd\omega

\displaystyle\longrightarrow i\phi_{2}=i\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dt{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{A}},t)\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\omega}\left(\frac{\beta\omega/2}{\tanh(\beta\omega/2)}-1\right)e^{-i\omega t}d\omega,

(S.103)

which yields Eq. (
S.91
). Here,
g_{\beta}(t)
is given by

\displaystyle g_{\beta}(t)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\omega}\left(\frac{\beta\omega/2}{\tanh(\beta\omega/2)}-1\right)e^{-i\omega t}d\omega.

(S.104)

This completes the proof
*1
*1

*1

The form of Eq. (
S.92
) can be derived by using the decomposition of

\displaystyle(x/2)\coth(x/2)=\frac{(x/2)}{\tanh(x/2)}=1+\sum_{m\neq 0}\frac{x}{x-2\pi im}

(S.105)

.
\square

S.II.1
Error bound for approximate quantum Belief propagation

Here, we consider the approximation of the belief propagation operator
\Phi_{{\mathcal{B}}}
in Eq. (
S.86
) as

\tilde{\Phi}_{{\mathcal{B}}}
as

\displaystyle\tilde{\Phi}_{{\mathcal{B}}}:=\mathcal{T}e^{\int_{0}^{1}\tilde{\phi}_{{\mathcal{B}},\tau}d\tau}.

(S.106)

The question here is whether or not we can obtain

\displaystyle\Phi_{{\mathcal{B}}}e^{\beta{\mathcal{A}}}\Phi_{{\mathcal{B}}}^{\dagger}\overset{\text{?}}{=}\tilde{\Phi}_{{\mathcal{B}}}e^{\beta{\mathcal{A}}}\tilde{\Phi}_{{\mathcal{B}}}^{\dagger}.

(S.107)

This kind of approximation is often critical in our analyses.
We prove the following lemma:

Lemma 9
.

Let us define
\tilde{\phi}_{{\mathcal{B}},\tau}
as in Eq. (
S.106
) such that
\|\phi_{{\mathcal{B}},\tau}-\tilde{\phi}_{{\mathcal{B}},\tau}\|\leq\delta
for
\forall\tau
.
Then, the norm difference between
\Phi_{{\mathcal{B}}}e^{\beta{\mathcal{A}}}\Phi_{{\mathcal{B}}}^{\dagger}
and
\tilde{\Phi}_{{\mathcal{B}}}e^{\beta{\mathcal{A}}}\tilde{\Phi}_{{\mathcal{B}}}
is upper-bounded by

\displaystyle\left\|\Phi_{{\mathcal{B}}}e^{\beta{\mathcal{A}}}\Phi_{{\mathcal{B}}}^{\dagger}-\tilde{\Phi}_{{\mathcal{B}}}e^{\beta{\mathcal{A}}}\tilde{\Phi}_{{\mathcal{B}}}^{\dagger}\right\|_{1}\leq 13e^{2\beta\left\|{\mathcal{B}}\right\|}\delta\left\|\Phi_{{\mathcal{B}}}e^{\beta{\mathcal{A}}}\Phi_{{\mathcal{B}}}^{\dagger}\right\|_{1}

(S.108)

as long as
\delta\leq 1
.

Proof of Lemma
9
.

We first note that the norm of operator
\phi_{{\mathcal{B}}}
is estimated by Eq. (
S.87
) as

\displaystyle\left\|\phi_{{\mathcal{B}},\tau}\right\|\leq\frac{\beta}{2}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f_{\beta}(t)\left\|{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{A}}_{\tau},t)\right\|dt\leq\frac{\beta\left\|{\mathcal{B}}\right\|}{2}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f_{\beta}(t)dt=\frac{\beta\left\|{\mathcal{B}}\right\|}{2},

(S.109)

where we use
f_{\beta}(t)\geq 0
and
\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f_{\beta}(t)dt=\tilde{f}(\omega=0)=1
from Eq. (
S.88
).
We thus obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\tilde{\phi}_{{\mathcal{B}},\tau}\right\|\leq\left\|\phi_{{\mathcal{B}},\tau}-\tilde{\phi}_{{\mathcal{B}},\tau}\right\|+\left\|\phi_{{\mathcal{B}},\tau}\right\|\leq\frac{\beta\left\|{\mathcal{B}}\right\|}{2}+\delta,

(S.110)

where we use the condition
\|\phi_{{\mathcal{B}},\tau}-\tilde{\phi}_{{\mathcal{B}},\tau}\|\leq\delta
.

To estimate the error in (
S.108
), we start from

\displaystyle\left\|\Phi_{{\mathcal{B}}}e^{\beta{\mathcal{A}}}\Phi_{{\mathcal{B}}}^{\dagger}-\tilde{\Phi}_{{\mathcal{B}}}e^{\beta{\mathcal{A}}}\tilde{\Phi}_{{\mathcal{B}}}^{\dagger}\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\left\|\Phi_{{\mathcal{B}}}e^{\beta{\mathcal{A}}}\Phi_{{\mathcal{B}}}^{\dagger}-\tilde{\Phi}_{{\mathcal{B}}}\Phi_{{\mathcal{B}}}^{-1}\Phi_{{\mathcal{B}}}e^{\beta{\mathcal{A}}}\Phi_{{\mathcal{B}}}^{\dagger}(\Phi_{{\mathcal{B}}}^{-1})^{\dagger}\tilde{\Phi}_{{\mathcal{B}}}^{\dagger}\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\Phi_{{\mathcal{B}}}e^{\beta{\mathcal{A}}}\Phi_{{\mathcal{B}}}^{\dagger}\right\|_{1}\left(2\left\|1-\tilde{\Phi}_{{\mathcal{B}}}\Phi_{{\mathcal{B}}}^{-1}\right\|+\left\|1-\tilde{\Phi}_{{\mathcal{B}}}\Phi_{{\mathcal{B}}}^{-1}\right\|^{2}\right).

(S.111)

The norm of
1-\tilde{\Phi}_{{\mathcal{B}}}\Phi_{{\mathcal{B}}}^{-1}
can be upper-bounded as

\displaystyle\left\|1-\tilde{\Phi}_{{\mathcal{B}}}\Phi_{{\mathcal{B}}}^{-1}\right\|\leq\left\|\Phi_{{\mathcal{B}}}^{-1}\right\|\cdot\left\|\Phi_{{\mathcal{B}}}-\tilde{\Phi}_{{\mathcal{B}}}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq e^{\beta\left\|{\mathcal{B}}\right\|/2}\cdot\delta\cdot e^{\delta+\beta\left\|{\mathcal{B}}\right\|/2},

(S.112)

where we use

\displaystyle\left\|\Phi_{{\mathcal{B}}}^{-1}\right\|\leq e^{\beta\left\|{\mathcal{B}}\right\|/2},\quad\left\|\Phi_{{\mathcal{B}}}\right\|\leq e^{\beta\left\|{\mathcal{B}}\right\|/2}

(S.113)

and the inequality as

\displaystyle\left\|\mathcal{T}e^{\int_{0}^{t}A(x)dx}-\mathcal{T}e^{\int_{0}^{t}B(x)dx}\right\|\leq\int_{0}^{t}\left\|\mathcal{T}e^{\int_{0}^{x}A(x)dx}\right\|\cdot\left\|A(x)-B(x)\right\|\cdot\left\|\mathcal{T}e^{\int_{x}^{t}B(x)dx}\right\|dx

(S.114)

for arbitrary operators
A(x)
and
B(x)
[see Ref.
[
116
, Eq. (32)]
for example].
Using the condition
\delta\leq 1
, we reduce the inequality (
S.112
) to

\displaystyle\left\|1-\tilde{\Phi}_{{\mathcal{B}}}\Phi_{{\mathcal{B}}}^{-1}\right\|\leq\delta e^{\beta\left\|{\mathcal{B}}\right\|+1},

(S.115)

which also yields

\displaystyle\left\|1-\tilde{\Phi}_{{\mathcal{B}}}\Phi_{{\mathcal{B}}}^{-1}\right\|^{2}\leq\delta^{2}e^{2\beta\left\|{\mathcal{B}}\right\|+2}\leq\delta e^{2\beta\left\|{\mathcal{B}}\right\|+2}.

(S.116)

By applying the above two inequalities to (
S.111
), we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\Phi_{{\mathcal{B}}}e^{\beta{\mathcal{A}}}\Phi_{{\mathcal{B}}}^{\dagger}-\tilde{\Phi}_{{\mathcal{B}}}e^{\beta{\mathcal{A}}}\tilde{\Phi}_{{\mathcal{B}}}^{\dagger}\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\Phi_{{\mathcal{B}}}e^{\beta{\mathcal{A}}}\Phi_{{\mathcal{B}}}^{\dagger}\right\|_{1}\delta\left(2e\cdot e^{\beta\left\|{\mathcal{B}}\right\|}+e^{2}e^{2\beta\left\|{\mathcal{B}}\right\|}\right)\leq 13e^{2\beta\left\|{\mathcal{B}}\right\|}\delta\left\|\Phi_{{\mathcal{B}}}e^{\beta{\mathcal{A}}}\Phi_{{\mathcal{B}}}^{\dagger}\right\|_{1},

which gives the main inequality (
S.108
). This completes the proof.
\square

[ End of Proof of Lemma
9
]

In various contexts, we often consider the decomposition of the Hamiltonian as

\displaystyle H=H_{L}+H_{L^{\rm c}}+\partial h_{L},

(S.117)

where
\partial h_{L}
has been defined as the surface interaction term as in Eq. (
S.9
).
We then set
{\mathcal{A}}=H_{L}+H_{L^{\rm c}}
and
{\mathcal{B}}=\partial h_{L}
in Eq. (
S.86
).
In this case,
\phi_{\partial h_{L},\tau}
is constructed by using the time evolution
\partial h_{L}(H_{L}+H_{L^{\rm c}}+\tau\partial h_{L},t)
.
Hence, we can utilize the Lieb–Robinson bound (
S.56
) to approximate
\phi_{\partial h_{L},\tau}
onto a subset
(\partial L)[r]
.
We prove the following statement:

Lemma 10
.

Under the decomposition (
S.117
), we define

\displaystyle\tilde{\phi}_{\partial h_{L},\tau}^{(r)}:=\tilde{{\rm tr}}_{(\partial L)[r]^{\rm c}}\left[\phi_{\partial h_{L},\tau}\right].

(S.118)

Then, for an arbitrary
\tau\in[0,1]
, we can prove

\displaystyle\left\|\phi_{\partial h_{L},\tau}-\tilde{\phi}_{\partial h_{L},\tau}^{(r)}\right\|\leq\bar{\phi}_{\beta,|\partial L|}e^{-\kappa_{\beta}r}

(S.119)

with

\displaystyle\bar{\phi}_{\beta,|\partial L|}:=4\beta\gamma\tilde{J}_{0}|\partial L|e^{\mu/2}\left[1+\frac{2\beta\gamma Cv|\partial L|}{7}\left(\frac{4D}{e\mu}\right)^{D}+\frac{8}{\pi^{2}}\log\left(e+\frac{e}{\kappa_{\beta}}\right)\frac{e^{\kappa_{\beta}}}{e^{\kappa_{\beta}}-1}\right],

(S.120)

where we define
\kappa_{\beta}
as follows:

\displaystyle\kappa_{\beta}:=\min\left(\frac{\pi\mu}{2v\beta},\frac{\mu}{4}\right).

(S.121)

Proof of Lemma
10
.

For an arbitrary unitary operator
u_{X}
acting on
X\subset\Lambda
, we consider

\displaystyle\left\|[\phi_{\partial h_{L},\tau},u_{X}]\right\|\leq\frac{\beta}{2}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f_{\beta}(t)\left\|[\partial h_{L}(H_{\tau},t),u_{X}]\right\|dt,\quad H_{\tau}:=H_{L}+H_{L^{\rm c}}+\tau\partial h_{L}.

(S.122)

By using Lemma
3
, we can obtain the upper bound for the LHS of Eq. (
S.119
) by choosing
X=(\partial L)[r]^{\rm c}
.
To estimate the quantity (
S.122
), we utilize Lemma
7
by choosing

\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}\to\partial L,\quad O\to\partial h_{L},\quad f(t)\to\frac{\beta}{2}f_{\beta}(t),\quad\Delta\ell\to 1.

(S.123)

Note that under the choice of
\mathfrak{L}\to\partial L
the summation in (
S.77
) with respect to
s
can be truncated up to
s=\ell
since
X_{s}
in Eq. (
S.79
) becomes the total set
\Lambda
for
s=\ell
, i.e.,
X_{\ell}=\left(\partial L[-1]\right)^{\rm c}=\Lambda
.

To utilize the inequality (
S.77
), we have to estimate an upper bound of

\displaystyle\left\|[\partial h_{L},u_{Y}\right\|\leq\begin{cases}\displaystyle 2\sum_{Z:Z\cap L\neq\emptyset,\ Z\cap Y\neq\emptyset}\left\|h_{Z}\right\|&\quad\textrm{for}\quad Y\subset L^{\rm c},\\
\displaystyle 2\sum_{Z:Z\cap L^{\rm c}\neq\emptyset,\ Z\cap Y\neq\emptyset}\left\|h_{Z}\right\|&\quad\textrm{for}\quad Y\subset L,\end{cases}

(S.124)

for unitary operators supported on
Y
such that
d_{Y,\partial L}=\ell
,
where we use the form of (
S.9
).
Using Lemma
1
with
X\to L
(
X\to L^{\rm c}
) and
Y\to L[\ell-1]^{\rm c}
(
Y\to L[-\ell]
), we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|[\partial h_{L},u_{(\partial L)[\ell-1]^{\rm c}}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{Z:Z\cap L\neq\emptyset,\ Z\cap L[\ell-1]^{\rm c}\neq\emptyset}\left\|h_{Z}\right\|+2\sum_{Z:Z\cap L^{\rm c}\neq\emptyset,\ Z\cap L[-\ell]\neq\emptyset}\left\|h_{Z}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq 2(|\partial L|+|\partial L^{\rm c}|)\tilde{J}_{0}e^{-\mu(\ell-1)/2}\leq 4\gamma\tilde{J}_{0}|\partial L|e^{-\mu(\ell-1)/2},

(S.125)

where we use
|\partial L^{\rm c}|\leq|\partial L[1]|\leq\gamma|\partial L|
.
Therefore, we can choose the quasi-locality function
\mathcal{F}(\ell)
in (
S.76
) as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(\ell)=4\gamma\tilde{J}_{0}|\partial L|e^{-\mu(\ell-1)/2}=:\tilde{J}_{L}e^{-\mu(\ell-1)/2},

(S.126)

where we use the fact that
d_{\partial L,(\partial L)[\ell-1]^{\rm c}}=\ell
.

We now have all the ingredients to estimate the RHS of the inequality (
S.77
), where we choose
\Delta\ell=1
.
We first estimate
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
, which are now calculated as

\displaystyle f_{1}=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\beta}{2}f_{\beta}(t)dt=\frac{\beta}{2},\quad f_{2}\leq\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\beta}{2}|t|f_{\beta}(t)dt=\frac{7\beta^{2}\zeta(3)}{2\pi^{3}}\leq\frac{\beta^{2}}{7},

(S.127)

where
\zeta(x)
is the Riemann zeta function, and we use the inequality of

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|t|f_{\beta}(t)dt=\frac{2}{\pi\beta}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|t|\log\left(\frac{e^{\pi|t|/\beta}+1}{e^{\pi|t|/\beta}-1}\right)dt=\frac{2}{\pi\beta}\cdot\frac{7\beta^{2}\zeta(3)}{2\pi^{2}}.

(S.128)

Also, for
f_{t_{0}}(s)
, we obtain by using (
S.89
)

\displaystyle f_{t_{0}}(s)=2\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}\frac{\beta}{2}f_{\beta}(t)dt\leq\frac{4}{\pi}\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}\frac{1}{e^{\pi|t|/\beta}-1}dt=\frac{4}{\pi}\cdot\frac{-1}{\pi/\beta}\log\left(1-e^{-\pi t_{0}/\beta}\right)\leq\frac{4\beta}{\pi^{2}}\log\left(e+\frac{e\beta}{\pi t_{0}}\right)e^{-\pi t_{0}/\beta},

(S.129)

where we use
-\log(1-e^{-x})\leq\log(e+e/x)e^{-x}
. Note that
t_{0}
is chosen as
t_{0}=\mu s/(2v)
for
\Delta\ell=1
in Lemma
7
.
We thus calculate the RHS of (
S.77
) as

\displaystyle 2f_{1}\mathcal{F}(\ell)+2\sum_{s=1}^{\ell}\mathcal{F}(\ell-s)\left[C|\partial(\partial L[\ell])|f_{2}ve^{-\mu s/2}+f_{t_{0}}(s)\right]

\displaystyle\leq 4\beta\gamma\tilde{J}_{0}|\partial L|e^{-\mu(\ell-1)/2}+\frac{2\beta^{2}\gamma Cv|\partial L|\ell^{D-1}}{7}4\gamma\tilde{J}_{0}|\partial L|\ell e^{-\mu(\ell-1)/2}+8\gamma\tilde{J}_{0}|\partial L|e^{\mu/2}\cdot\frac{4\beta}{\pi^{2}}\log\left(e+\frac{e}{\kappa_{\beta}}\right)\frac{e^{\kappa_{\beta}}}{e^{\kappa_{\beta}}-1}e^{-\kappa_{\beta}\ell}

\displaystyle\leq 4\beta\gamma\tilde{J}_{0}|\partial L|e^{\mu/2}e^{-\kappa_{\beta}\ell}\left(1+\frac{2\beta\gamma Cv|\partial L|}{7}\ell^{D}e^{-\mu\ell/4}+\frac{8}{\pi^{2}}\log\left(e+\frac{e}{\kappa_{\beta}}\right)\frac{e^{\kappa_{\beta}}}{e^{\kappa_{\beta}}-1}\right),

(S.130)

where we use
|\partial(X[\ell])|\leq\gamma\ell^{D-1}|X|
for
\forall X\subset\Lambda
, the definition (
S.121
) for
\kappa_{\beta}
,

\mu/2\geq 2\kappa_{\beta}
(
\mu/4\geq\kappa_{\beta}
) and
\pi t_{0}/\beta=\pi\mu s/(2v\beta)\geq\kappa_{\beta}s
to derive the inequalities of

\displaystyle\sum_{s=1}^{\ell}\mathcal{F}(\ell-s)e^{-\mu s/2}\leq 4\gamma\tilde{J}_{0}|\partial L|\sum_{s=1}^{\ell}e^{-\mu(\ell-s-1)/2}\cdot e^{-\mu s/2}=4\gamma\tilde{J}_{0}|\partial L|\ell e^{-\mu(\ell-1)/2},

(S.131)

and

\displaystyle\sum_{s=1}^{\ell}\mathcal{F}(\ell-s)f_{t_{0}}(s)

\displaystyle\leq 4\gamma\tilde{J}_{0}|\partial L|e^{\mu/2}\sum_{s=1}^{\ell}e^{-2\kappa_{\beta}(\ell-s)}\cdot\frac{4\beta}{\pi^{2}}\log\left(e+\frac{e}{\kappa_{\beta}s}\right)e^{-\kappa_{\beta}s}

\displaystyle\leq 4\gamma\tilde{J}_{0}|\partial L|e^{\mu/2}\cdot\frac{4\beta}{\pi^{2}}\log\left(e+\frac{e}{\kappa_{\beta}}\right)\frac{e^{\kappa_{\beta}}}{e^{\kappa_{\beta}}-1}e^{-\kappa_{\beta}\ell}.

(S.132)

Now, Eq. (
S.122
) is upper-bounded by (
S.130
) by letting
\ell=r
.
Therefore, by applying

\displaystyle\ell^{D}e^{-\mu\ell/4}\leq\left(\frac{4D}{e\mu}\right)^{D}

(S.133)

to (
S.130
), we prove the main inequality (
S.119
),
where we use
x^{m}e^{-\mu x/4}\leq\left[4m/(e\mu)\right]^{m}
for
m\in\mathbb{N}
.
This completes the proof.
\square

[ End of Proof of Lemma
10
]

From Lemmas
8
,
9
and
10
, we immediately prove the following corollary:

Corollary 11
.

Let
L
be a connected region and
\tilde{\Phi}_{\partial h_{L}}^{(r)}
be defined as

\displaystyle\tilde{\Phi}_{\partial h_{L}}^{(r)}:=\mathcal{T}e^{\int_{0}^{1}\tilde{\phi}_{\partial h_{L},\tau}^{(r)}d\tau}.

(S.134)

Then, using
\tilde{\Phi}_{\partial h_{L}}^{(r)}
as the belief propagation operator instead of
\Phi_{\partial h_{L}}
, we obtain the error as follows:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{Z_{\beta}}\left\|e^{\beta H}-\tilde{\Phi}_{\partial h_{L}}^{(r)}e^{\beta\left(H_{L}+H_{L^{\rm c}}\right)}\tilde{\Phi}_{\partial h_{L}}^{(r)\dagger}\right\|_{1}\leq 13\bar{\phi}_{\beta,|\partial L|}e^{2\beta\left\|\partial h_{L}\right\|-\kappa_{\beta}r}.

(S.135)

Remark.
By using the error bound of (
S.135
), the error is sufficiently small under the condition that

\displaystyle r\gtrsim\frac{2\beta}{\kappa_{\beta}}\left\|\partial h_{L}\right\|=\mathcal{O}(\beta^{2})|\partial L|,

(S.136)

where we use
\kappa_{\beta}=\mathcal{O}(1/\beta)
from Eq. (
S.121
) and

\left\|\partial h_{L}\right\|\leq\sum_{Z:Z\cap\mathfrak{L}\neq\emptyset,\ Z\cap L^{\rm c}\neq\emptyset}\left\|h_{Z}\right\|\leq|\partial L|\mathcal{J}_{0}=\tilde{J}_{0}|\partial L|
from the inequality (
S.17
) in Lemma
1
.
In one-dimensional systems, the cardinality
|\partial L|
is
\mathcal{O}(1)
and hence the condition reduces to
r\gtrsim\mathcal{O}(\beta^{2})
.
On the other hand, in higher dimensions, the cardinality
|\partial L|
is as large as
[{\rm diam}(L)]^{D-1}
, i.e.,

r\gtrsim\beta^{2}|\partial L|\approx\beta^{2}[{\rm diam}(L)]^{D-1}
.
This point prohibits us from utilizing the belief propagation in estimating the conditional mutual information as in Sec.
S.III.2
in dimensions greater than one
*2
*2

*2

This point might be improved by cleverly employing the quasi-locality of the Hamiltonian. However, as far as we know, there are no results that improve the bound as in (
S.135
) at arbitrary temperatures.
.

Figure 9:
Approximate belief propagation operator in high dimensions (2D picture). When we consider the belief propagation operator
\Phi_{\partial h_{L}}
for the decomposition of
H=H_{L}+H_{L^{\rm c}}
, the local approximation by
\Phi_{\partial h_{L}}\approx\tilde{\Phi}_{\partial h_{L}}^{(r)}
holds for
r\gtrsim\beta^{2}|\partial L|
from the inequality (
S.135
).
Therefore, the approximate belief propagation operator
\tilde{\Phi}_{\partial h_{L}}^{(r)}
is completely non-local regardless of the size of
L
as long as we look at it in the region
L
.
This becomes a primary bottleneck in applying the belief propagation technique in a high-dimensional Gibbs state.

S.III
Effective Hamiltonian theory on subsystem

In this section, we consider the entanglement Hamiltonian that is defined by the partial trace operation for a subset
L\subset\Lambda
:

\displaystyle{\rm tr}_{L}\left(e^{\beta H}\right)=e^{\beta H^{\ast}_{L^{\rm c}}}.

(S.137)

When considering the effective interaction term
H^{\ast}_{L^{\rm c}}-H_{L^{\rm c}}
, the problem is whether such interaction approximately localizes around the boundary of
L
.
This kind of analysis is the crucial ingredient to treating the quantum conditional mutual information (see also Lemma
14
and Corollary
17
below).
In the case where the Hamiltonian is commuting and has an interaction length up to
k
, it is clear that the effective interaction is strictly localized around
\partial L
.
The proof is immediately given as follows:

\displaystyle{\rm tr}_{L}\left(e^{\beta H}\right)={\rm tr}_{L}\left(e^{\beta H_{L^{\rm c}}}e^{\beta\left(H_{L}+\partial h_{L}\right)}\right)=e^{\beta H_{L^{\rm c}}}{\rm tr}_{L}\left(e^{\beta\left(H_{L}+\partial h_{L}\right)}\right)=e^{\beta H_{L^{\rm c}}+\log\left\{{\rm tr}_{L}\left[e^{\beta\left(H_{L}+\partial h_{L}\right)}\right]\right\}},

(S.138)

which gives
H^{\ast}_{L^{\rm c}}=H_{L^{\rm c}}+(1/\beta)\log\left\{{\rm tr}_{L}\left[e^{\beta\left(H_{L}+\partial h_{L}\right)}\right]\right\}
.
Note that the support of
\partial h_{L}
is included in
L[k]
from the assumption on the interaction length. Therefore,
{\rm tr}_{L}\left[e^{\beta\left(H_{L}+\partial h_{L}\right)}\right]
as well as

\log\left\{{\rm tr}_{L}\left[e^{\beta\left(H_{L}+\partial h_{L}\right)}\right]\right\}
is also supported on
L[k]\setminus L
.
Thus, we conclude that the effective interaction is localized up to a distance
k
.

The challenge here is to generalize the statement to non-commuting Hamiltonians.
So far, no established theoretical framework exists to analyze the entanglement Hamiltonian after partial trace.
In the following, we show two formalisms that rely on quantum belief propagation (BP formalism, see Sec.
S.III.2
) and partial-trace projection (PTP formalism, see Sec.
S.III.3
).
The former is utilized in one-dimensional systems, while the latter is applied to high-dimensional systems.
We note that in the 1D case, the result becomes better when we use the BP formalism.

S.III.1
Norm of the entanglement Hamiltonian

Before going to the quasi-locality, we first derive a fundamental statement on the norm of
\beta H^{\ast}_{L^{\rm c}}=\log\left[{\rm tr}_{L}\left(\rho_{\beta}\right)\right]=\log(\rho_{\beta,L^{\rm c}})
, where we use the notation (
S.12
).
At first glance, it is a trivial problem to prove

\displaystyle\left\|\beta H^{\ast}_{L^{\rm c}}\right\|=\mathcal{O}(\beta|L^{\rm c}|).

(S.139)

Indeed, in the commuting cases, one can easily derive it by using

\displaystyle e^{\beta H}=e^{\beta H_{L}+\beta\widehat{H_{L^{\rm c}}}}\succeq e^{\beta H_{L}-\beta\left\|\widehat{H_{L^{\rm c}}}\right\|},

(S.140)

which yields

\displaystyle\frac{1}{Z_{\beta}}{\rm tr}_{L}\left(e^{\beta H}\right)\succeq\frac{{\rm tr}_{L}\left(e^{\beta H_{L}}\right)}{Z_{\beta}}e^{-\beta\left\|\widehat{H_{L^{\rm c}}}\right\|}\succeq e^{-2\beta\left\|\widehat{H_{L^{\rm c}}}\right\|-\log(\mathcal{D}_{L^{\rm c}})},

(S.141)

where we use the notation (
S.8
), i.e.,
\widehat{H_{L^{\rm c}}}=H_{L^{\rm c}}+\partial h_{L}
, and the last inequality is derived from the Golden-Thompson inequality as

\displaystyle Z_{\beta}={\rm tr}\left(e^{\beta H_{L}+\beta\widehat{H_{L^{\rm c}}}}\right)\leq{\rm tr}\left(e^{\beta H_{L}}e^{\beta\widehat{H_{L^{\rm c}}}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq e^{\beta\left\|\widehat{H_{L^{\rm c}}}\right\|}{\rm tr}\left(e^{\beta H_{L}}\otimes\hat{1}_{L^{\rm c}}\right)=e^{\beta\left\|\widehat{H_{L^{\rm c}}}\right\|}\mathcal{D}_{L^{\rm c}}{\rm tr}_{L}\left(e^{\beta H_{L}}\right).

(S.142)

Note that
\mathcal{D}_{L^{\rm c}}
is the Hilbert space dimension on
L^{\rm c}
.
From the upper bound
\left\|\widehat{H_{L^{\rm c}}}\right\|\leq\bar{J}_{0}|L^{\rm c}|
which is derived from the condition (
S.6
), we reduce the inequality (
S.141
) to the desired one (
S.139
) as follows:

\displaystyle\left\|\beta H^{\ast}_{L^{\rm c}}\right\|\leq 2\beta\left\|\widehat{H_{L^{\rm c}}}\right\|+\log(\mathcal{D}_{L^{\rm c}})\leq 2\beta\bar{J}_{0}|L^{\rm c}|+\log(\mathcal{D}_{L^{\rm c}}).

(S.143)

The difficulty in the non-commuting case originates from the fact that the operator inequality (
S.140
) does not generally hold.
Indeed, from Ref.
[
77
, Lemma 12]
, we ensure

\displaystyle e^{\beta H}=e^{\beta H_{L}+\beta\widehat{H_{L^{\rm c}}}}\succeq e^{\beta H_{L}-\zeta}

(S.144)

only when
\zeta\geq\beta\left\|\widehat{H_{L^{\rm c}}}\right\|+\beta\left\|H_{L}\right\|=\Omega(\beta|\Lambda|)
.
This condition is proven to be qualitatively tight.
Hence, the inequality (
S.144
) leads to a rather weaker bound of
\log\left[{\rm tr}_{L}\left(e^{\beta H}\right)/Z_{\beta}\right]=\beta\mathcal{O}(|\Lambda|)
.
By employing the techniques in Ref.
[
65
]
based on
[
120
]
, we can prove the desired bound (
S.139
) for general quantum Gibbs states.

Proposition 12
.

Under the condition that the Hamiltonian is
k
-local as

\displaystyle H=\sum_{Z:|Z|\leq k}h_{Z},\quad\max_{i\in\Lambda}\sum_{Z:Z\ni i}\|h_{Z}\|\leq\bar{J}_{0},

(S.145)

we have

\displaystyle\left\|\beta H^{\ast}_{L^{\rm c}}\right\|\leq\beta\bar{J}_{0}|L^{\rm c}|+\log(16\bar{J}_{0}|L^{\rm c}|\mathcal{D}_{L^{\rm c}})=\mathcal{O}(\beta|L^{\rm c}|),

(S.146)

where
\bar{J}_{0}
is defined by the inequality of

\displaystyle 3J_{0}|L^{\rm c}|+(2J_{0}k)\log(8\mathcal{D}_{L^{\rm c}})+1\leq\bar{J}_{0}|L^{\rm c}|.

(S.147)

Remark.
From the statement, as long as the Hamiltonian is
k
-local, we obtain qualitatively the same upper bound as the one in the commuting cases (
S.143
). On the other hand, as the
k
increases, the upper bound linearly increases with
k
.
For
L^{\rm c}=\mathcal{O}(1)
, our result implies that the entanglement Hamiltonian on
L^{\rm c}
has an amplitude of
\mathcal{O}(1)
. Hence, only a small portion of the global interaction terms in
H_{L}
contributes to the entanglement Hamiltonian.

In our definition of the Hamiltonian in Eq. (
S.6
), we do not assume the strict
k
-locality.
Without the strict
k
-locality, we can prove a similar but weaker inequality to (
S.146
) as long as the bound (
S.161
) in Ref.
[
120
]
is given (see below).
Using Ref.
[
65
, Supplementary Lemma 9]
, we can obtain

\displaystyle g_{\Delta,X}\leq\Theta(|X|)e^{-\Theta(1)(\Delta/|X|)^{\mathfrak{u}/(2-\mathfrak{u})}}

(S.148)

under the similar condition to (
S.7
):

\displaystyle\sum_{Z:Z\ni\{i,i^{\prime}\}}\left\|h_{Z}\right\|\leq\bar{J}_{0}e^{-\mu d_{i,i^{\prime}}^{\mathfrak{u}}}\quad(0\leq\mathfrak{u}\leq 1),

(S.149)

where the case of
\mathfrak{u}=1
corresponds to the condition (
S.7
).
Then, in the inequality (
S.166
), we need to choose the parameter
\Delta
as

\displaystyle\Delta=\Theta(|L^{\rm c}|)\left[\log(\mathcal{D}_{L^{\rm c}})\right]^{(2-\mathfrak{u})/\mathfrak{u}},

(S.150)

which yields

\displaystyle\left\|\beta H^{\ast}_{L^{\rm c}}\right\|=\mathcal{O}\left(\beta|L^{\rm c}|^{2/\mathfrak{u}}\right).

(S.151)

This is still polynomial concerning the subsystem size
|L^{\rm c}|
.

As a simple application, we consider the following entanglement Hamiltonian learning on a small subsystem, which will also be utilized for the global Hamiltonian learning in one-dimensional systems (see Sec.
S.X.1
):

Corollary 13
.

Let us adopt the same setup as in Proposition
12
.
Then, given
N
copies of the quantum Gibbs state
\rho_{\beta}
, one can reconstruct an entanglement Hamiltonian
\log(\sigma_{X})
up to the following error with the success probability larger than
0.99
:

\displaystyle\left\|\log(\sigma_{X})-\log(\rho_{\beta,X})\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\log(N/20)}{N}920\mathcal{D}_{X}^{2}(16\bar{J}_{0}|X|\mathcal{D}_{X})^{3/2}e^{3\beta\bar{J}_{0}|X|/2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\log(N)}{N}e^{\Theta(\beta)|X|},

(S.152)

where we assume
N\geq 40
.

Proof of Corollary
13
.
Using the result in Ref.
[
156
, Theorem 1]
, the sufficient number of copies to reconstruct
\rho_{\beta,X}
up to an error
\epsilon
is
upper-bounded by

\displaystyle N\leq 20(\mathcal{D}_{X}^{2}/\epsilon)\log(\mathcal{D}_{X}^{2}/\epsilon),

(S.153)

where the success probability is larger than
0.99
.
Here, the error is estimated by using the trace norm; that is, the reconstructed state
\sigma_{X}
satisfies

\displaystyle\left\|\sigma_{X}-\rho_{\beta,X}\right\|_{1}\leq\epsilon.

(S.154)

By solving the inequality (
S.153
), we have

\displaystyle(\mathcal{D}_{X}^{2}/\epsilon)\geq\frac{(N/20)}{W(N/20)}\longrightarrow\epsilon\leq\frac{40\mathcal{D}_{X}^{2}\log(N/20)}{N},

(S.155)

where we use
N\geq 40
and
W(x)\leq 2\log(x)
for
x\geq 2
.
Note that the function
W(x)
is the Lambert W function and is defined by
W(x)e^{W(x)}=x
.

Also, by combining the inequalities (
S.740
) and (
S.750
) in Theorem
7
below, we prove

\displaystyle\left\|\log(\sigma_{X})-\log(\rho_{\beta,X})\right\|\leq\frac{\left\|\sigma_{X}-\rho_{\beta,X}\right\|}{\lambda^{-1}_{\min}}\left[\frac{4\log(2\lambda^{-1}_{\min})}{\pi}\log\left(\frac{e\log(2\lambda^{-1}_{\min})}{2\pi}\right)+23\right]\leq\frac{23\epsilon}{\lambda^{-3/2}_{\min}},

(S.156)

where we let
\lambda_{\rm min}
be the minimum eigenvalue of
\rho_{\beta,X}
, and the operator norm is smaller than the trace norm, i.e.,
\left\|\sigma_{X}-\rho_{\beta,X}\right\|\leq\left\|\sigma_{X}-\rho_{\beta,X}\right\|_{1}\leq\epsilon
.
By choosing
L^{\rm c}
as
X
in the upper bound (
S.146
), we prove

\displaystyle\lambda^{-3/2}_{\min}\leq e^{(3/2)\left\|\beta H^{\ast}_{X}\right\|}\leq(16\bar{J}_{0}|X|\mathcal{D}_{X})^{3/2}e^{3\beta\bar{J}_{0}|X|/2}.

(S.157)

By combining the inequalities (
S.155
), (
S.156
) and (
S.157
), we prove the main inequality (
S.152
).
This completes the proof of Corollary
13
.
\square

S.III.1.1
Proof of Proposition
12

For the proof, we aim to derive the lower bound on the minimum eigenvalue of
\rho_{\beta,L^{\rm c}}
, which we denote by
\lambda_{\min}

Because of
\rho_{\beta,L^{\rm c}}\preceq\hat{1}_{L^{\rm c}}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\log(\rho_{\beta,L^{\rm c}})\right\|=\log(1/\lambda_{\min}),

(S.158)

and it also gives the upper bound of
\left\|\beta H^{\ast}_{L^{\rm c}}\right\|
.
To derive the minimum eigenvalue
\lambda_{\min}
, we consider

\displaystyle\lambda_{\min}=\inf_{P_{L^{\rm c}}}\left[{\rm tr}_{L^{\rm c}}(P_{L^{\rm c}}\rho_{\beta,L^{\rm c}})\right]=\inf_{P_{L^{\rm c}}}\left[{\rm tr}\left(P_{L^{\rm c}}\rho_{\beta}\right)\right].

(S.159)

For the estimation of
\lambda_{\min}
, we consider an arbitrary projector
P
and estimate

\displaystyle\delta={\rm tr}(P\rho_{\beta})=\left\|P\sqrt{\rho_{\beta}}\right\|_{F}.

(S.160)

To apply the techniques in Ref.
[
65
]
, we define the projection onto the energy eigenspace with the energies within
[E,\infty)
and
(-\infty,E]
as
\Pi_{\geq E}
and
\Pi_{\leq E}
, respectively.
We then denote the parameter
g_{\Delta,X}
which satisfies the following inequality:

\displaystyle\sup_{O_{X}:\left\|O_{X}\right\|=1}\left\|\Pi_{\geq E+\Delta}O_{X}\Pi_{\leq E}\right\|\leq g_{\Delta,X}\quad\textrm{for}\quad\forall E\in\mathbb{R}.

(S.161)

The parameter
g_{\Delta,X}
characterizes the robustness of the energy spectrum to local operators and has been given by
[
120
]

\displaystyle g_{\Delta,X}=e^{-(\Delta-3J_{0}|X|)/(4J_{0}k)},

(S.162)

where the above expression was given in
[
157
, Theorem 3.2]
.

Using
g_{\Delta,X}
, we generally obtain the following inequality on the robustness of the expectation of
P
to the local unitary operator
U_{X}
:

\displaystyle\left\|PU_{X}\sqrt{\rho_{\beta}}\right\|_{F}\leq(4\Delta+2)e^{\beta(\Delta+1)}\delta+2g_{\Delta,X}^{2}\quad\textrm{for}\quad\forall\Delta>0,

(S.163)

where the proof was given in Ref.
[
65
, Supplementary inequality (116)]
.
The interpretation of the inequality (
S.163
) is that local unitary operators do not significantly influence the expectation value of
P
.

Moreover, if
P
is supported on
X
, i.e.,
P=P_{X}
, there exists a unitary operator
\check{U}_{X}
to reduce
\left\|P\check{U}_{X}\sqrt{\rho_{\beta}}\right\|_{F}
to the infinite-temperature average such that
[
65
, Claim 36]

\displaystyle\left\|P\check{U}_{X}\sqrt{\rho_{\beta}}\right\|_{F}\geq\frac{1}{2}\left\|P_{X}\sqrt{\rho_{\beta=0}}\right\|_{F}\geq\frac{{\rm tr}_{X}(P_{X})}{2\mathcal{D}_{X}}.

(S.164)

By combining (
S.163
) and (
S.164
), we arrive at the inequality of

\displaystyle\delta\geq\frac{e^{-\beta(\Delta+1)}}{4(\Delta+1)}\left(\frac{{\rm tr}_{X}(P_{X})}{2\mathcal{D}_{X}}-2g_{\Delta,X}^{2}\right)\quad\textrm{for}\quad\forall\Delta>0.

(S.165)

Therefore, by applying the inequality (
S.165
) to Eq. (
S.159
) with
X=L^{\rm c}
and
{\rm tr}_{L^{\rm c}}\left(P_{L^{\rm c}}\right)=1
, we prove the lower bound of

\displaystyle\lambda_{\min}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{e^{-\beta(\Delta+1)}}{4(\Delta+1)}\left(\frac{1}{2\mathcal{D}_{L^{\rm c}}}-2e^{-(\Delta-3J_{0}|L^{\rm c}|)/(2J_{0}k)}\right)\geq\frac{e^{-\beta\bar{J}_{0}|L^{\rm c}|}}{16\mathcal{D}_{L^{\rm c}}\bar{J}_{0}|L^{\rm c}|},

(S.166)

where we choose
\Delta=3J_{0}|L^{\rm c}|+(2J_{0}k)\log(8\mathcal{D}_{L^{\rm c}})
and use
\Delta+1\leq\bar{J}_{0}|L^{\rm c}|
from the definition of
\bar{J}_{0}
in Eq. (
S.147
).
We thus prove the main inequality (
S.146
).
This completes the proof of Proposition
12
.
\square

S.III.2
Belief propagation formalism

Figure 10:
Schematic picture of the Belief Propagation (BP) formalism. Let’s decompose the total system into
\Lambda=L\cup X\cup Y
as given in Eq. (
S.167
). We begin with the approximation of the belief propagation operator between the regions
LX
and
Y
so that it can be supported on
X
and
Y
, which is denoted by
\tilde{\Phi}_{XY}
. Then, for the approximated Gibbs state
\tilde{\Phi}_{XY}e^{\beta(H_{LX}+H_{L[\ell]^{\rm c}})}\tilde{\Phi}_{XY}^{\dagger}
, we can perform the partial trace with respect to
L
without influencing the region
Y
, i.e.,

{\rm tr}_{L}\left[\tilde{\Phi}_{XY}e^{\beta(H_{LX}+H_{L[\ell]^{\rm c}})}\tilde{\Phi}_{XY}^{\dagger}\right]=\tilde{\Phi}_{XY}{\rm tr}_{L}\left[e^{\beta H_{LX}}\right]\otimes e^{\beta H_{L[\ell]^{\rm c}}}\tilde{\Phi}_{XY}^{\dagger}
.
Therefore, we need to calculate the entanglement Hamiltonian of
\tilde{\Phi}_{XY}e^{\beta(\tilde{H}^{\ast}_{X}+H_{L[\ell]^{\rm c}})}\tilde{\Phi}_{XY}^{\dagger}
, where
\tilde{H}^{\ast}_{X}
is defined by the partial trace for
e^{\beta H_{LX}}
. Subsequently, the problem now reduces to estimating the quasi-locality for connections of exponential operators (see Sec.
S.IV
).

From this section, we consider the construction of the entanglement Hamiltonian.
We first consider a tripartition of the total system as
\Lambda=L\sqcup X\sqcup Y
(see Fig.
10
), where we define

\displaystyle X=L[\ell]\setminus L,\quad Y=L[\ell]^{\rm c},\quad X\sqcup Y=L^{\rm c}.

(S.167)

Then, the quantum belief propagation for the bipartition of the Hamiltonian as
H=H_{L[\ell]}+H_{L[\ell]^{\rm c}}+\partial h_{L[\ell]}
gives

\displaystyle e^{\beta H}=\Phi_{\partial h_{L[\ell]}}e^{\beta\left(H_{LX}+H_{L[\ell]^{\rm c}}\right)}\Phi_{\partial h_{L[\ell]}}^{\dagger},

(S.168)

where we denote
L[\ell]=LX
.
We consider the approximation of
\Phi_{\partial h_{L[\ell]}}
onto the
XY
, which we denote by
\tilde{\Phi}_{XY}
.
By the notation of Corollary
11
,
\tilde{\Phi}_{XY}
is described by
\tilde{\Phi}_{\partial h_{L[\ell]}}^{(\ell-1)}
, and hence we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{Z_{\beta}}\left\|e^{\beta H}-\tilde{\Phi}_{XY}e^{\beta\left(H_{LX}+H_{L[\ell]^{\rm c}}\right)}\tilde{\Phi}_{XY}^{\dagger}\right\|_{1}\leq 13\bar{\phi}_{\beta,|\partial L[\ell]|}\left\|\partial h_{L[\ell]}\right\|e^{2\beta\left\|\partial h_{L[\ell]}\right\|-\kappa_{\beta}(\ell-1)},

(S.169)

where we use the inequality (
S.135
).
The above inequality immediately yields

\displaystyle\frac{1}{Z_{\beta}}\left\|{\rm tr}_{L}\left(e^{\beta H}\right)-\tilde{\Phi}_{XY}{\rm tr}_{L}\left[e^{\beta\left(H_{LX}+H_{L[\ell]^{\rm c}}\right)}\right]\tilde{\Phi}_{XY}^{\dagger}\right\|_{1}\leq 13\bar{\phi}_{\beta,|\partial L[\ell]|}\left\|\partial h_{L[\ell]}\right\|e^{2\beta\left\|\partial h_{L[\ell]}\right\|-\kappa_{\beta}(\ell-1)}.

(S.170)

Hence, by defining

\displaystyle{\rm tr}_{L}\left[e^{\beta\left(H_{LX}+H_{L[\ell]^{\rm c}}\right)}\right]={\rm tr}_{L}\left(e^{\beta H_{LX}}\right)e^{\beta H_{L[\ell]^{\rm c}}}=:e^{\beta\tilde{H}^{\ast}_{X}}e^{\beta H_{L[\ell]^{\rm c}}},

(S.171)

we reduce the inequality (
S.170
) to

\displaystyle\frac{1}{Z_{\beta}}\left\|e^{\beta H^{\ast}_{L^{\rm c}}}-\tilde{\Phi}_{XY}e^{\beta\left(\tilde{H}^{\ast}_{X}+H_{L[\ell]^{\rm c}}\right)}\tilde{\Phi}_{XY}^{\dagger}\right\|_{1}\leq 13\bar{\phi}_{\beta,|\partial L[\ell]|}\left\|\partial h_{L[\ell]}\right\|e^{2\beta\left\|\partial h_{L[\ell]}\right\|-\kappa_{\beta}(\ell-1)},

(S.172)

where we use
[H_{LX},H_{L[\ell]^{\rm c}}]=0
in defining the effective Hamiltonian
\tilde{H}^{\ast}_{X}
.
Note that we cannot obtain any information on the structure of
\tilde{H}^{\ast}_{X}
except that it is supported on
X
.
Subsequently, we need to consider the entanglement Hamiltonian of

\displaystyle\tilde{\Phi}_{XY}e^{\beta\left(\tilde{H}^{\ast}_{X}+H_{L[\ell]^{\rm c}}\right)}\tilde{\Phi}_{XY}^{\dagger}=\mathcal{T}e^{\int_{0}^{1}\tilde{\phi}_{XY,\tau}d\tau}e^{\beta\left(\tilde{H}^{\ast}_{X}+H_{L[\ell]^{\rm c}}\right)}\left(\mathcal{T}e^{\int_{0}^{1}\tilde{\phi}_{XY,\tau}d\tau}\right)^{\dagger},

(S.173)

with

\displaystyle\tilde{\Phi}_{XY}:=\mathcal{T}e^{\int_{0}^{1}\tilde{\phi}_{XY,\tau}d\tau},

(S.174)

which necessitates us to estimate the quasi-locality of the entanglement Hamiltonian due to the connection of exponential operators (see Sec.
S.IV
).
Note that
\left\|\partial h_{L[\ell]}\right\|\propto\ell^{D-1}
, and hence we cannot obtain a good approximation in (
S.172
) for dimensions higher than
2
.

As the second question, we are interested in whether we can efficiently approximate the conditional mutual information.
The straightforward way is to utilize the continuity inequality
[
158
]
.
By denoting

\displaystyle\tilde{\rho}_{\beta}:=\frac{\tilde{\Phi}_{XY}e^{\beta\left(H_{LX}+H_{L[\ell]^{\rm c}}\right)}\tilde{\Phi}_{XY}}{{\rm tr}\left(\tilde{\Phi}_{XY}e^{\beta\left(H_{LX}+H_{L[\ell]^{\rm c}}\right)}\tilde{\Phi}_{XY}\right)},\quad\delta_{\beta}:=\left\|\rho_{\beta}-\tilde{\rho}_{\beta}\right\|_{1},

(S.175)

one can derive

\displaystyle\left|{\mathcal{I}}_{\rho_{\beta}}(A:C|B)-{\mathcal{I}}_{\tilde{\rho}_{\beta}}(A:C|B)\right|\leq\delta_{\beta}\log\left[\min(\mathcal{D}_{A},\mathcal{D}_{C})\right]+\left(1+\frac{\delta_{\beta}}{2}\right)\left(\frac{\delta_{\beta}}{2+\delta_{\beta}}\right),

(S.176)

where
h(x)=-x\log(x)-(1-x)\log(1-x)
with
0\leq x\leq 1
.
However, when we consider the thermodynamic limit as
|\Lambda|\to\infty
, the above upper bound may not be utilized since
\min(\mathcal{D}_{A},\mathcal{D}_{C})
can be infinitely large.
To resolve this issue, we prove the following lemma:

Lemma 14
.

Let us define
H_{\rho}(A:C|B)
as

\displaystyle H_{\rho}(A:C|B)=-\log(\rho_{AB})-\log(\rho_{BC})+\log(\rho_{ABC})+\log(\rho_{B})

(S.177)

for an arbitrary quantum state
\rho
. Here, we do not assume that
A
,
B
and
C
constitute the total system, i.e.,

\displaystyle A\cup B\cup C\subseteq\Lambda.

(S.178)

Then, we obtain

\displaystyle{\mathcal{I}}_{\rho_{\beta}}(A:C|B)\leq\left\|H_{\tilde{\rho}_{\beta}}(A:C|B)\right\|+4\left\|\beta H-\log\left(\tilde{\Phi}_{XY}e^{\beta\left(H_{LX}+H_{L[\ell]^{\rm c}}\right)}\tilde{\Phi}_{XY}^{\dagger}\right)\right\|+4\delta_{\beta,\ell,L},

(S.179)

where we define
\delta_{\beta,\ell,L}
by (
S.169
) as follows:

\displaystyle\delta_{\beta,\ell,L}:=13\bar{\phi}_{\beta,|\partial L[\ell]|}\left\|\partial h_{L[\ell]}\right\|e^{2\beta\left\|\partial h_{L[\ell]}\right\|-\kappa_{\beta}(\ell-1)}.

(S.180)

Remark.

The second term in the RHS of (
S.179
) is close to the norm difference of

\left\|\log(\rho_{\beta})-\log(\tilde{\rho}_{\beta})\right\|
.
Although we can ensure
\rho_{\beta}\approx\tilde{\rho}_{\beta}
, the logarithm of the operator is upper-bounded by using the minimum eigenvalue
\lambda_{\min}
of
\rho_{\beta}
[see the inequality (
S.156
)]:

\displaystyle\left\|\log(\rho_{\beta})-\log(\tilde{\rho}_{\beta})\right\|\lesssim\left\|\rho_{\beta}-\tilde{\rho}_{\beta}\right\|/\lambda_{\min}=e^{\mathcal{O}(|\Lambda|)}\left\|\rho_{\beta}-\tilde{\rho}_{\beta}\right\|,

(S.181)

which necessitates the exponentially accurate error between
\rho_{\beta}
and
\tilde{\rho}_{\beta}
to ensure the good approximation for
\left\|\log(\rho_{\beta})-\log(\tilde{\rho}_{\beta})\right\|
. This has been a main bottleneck to the quantum Hamiltonian learning
[
65
]
.
In Proposition
20
, we will give a much better error bound between
\log(\rho_{\beta})
and
\log(\tilde{\rho}_{\beta})
.

S.III.2.1
Proof of Lemma
14

We start from the following inequality that holds for arbitrarily reduced density matrices
\rho_{L^{\rm c}}
and
\tilde{\rho}_{L^{\rm c}}
:

\displaystyle S\left(\rho_{L^{\rm c}}|\tilde{\rho}_{L^{\rm c}}\right):={\rm tr}\left[\rho_{L^{\rm c}}\log(\rho_{L^{\rm c}})-\rho_{L^{\rm c}}\log(\tilde{\rho}_{L^{\rm c}})\right]\leq S\left(\rho|\tilde{\rho}\right)={\rm tr}\left[\rho\log(\rho)-\rho\log(\tilde{\rho})\right],

(S.182)

which is derived from the monotonicity of the quantum relative entropy
[
159
,
160
,
161
]
.
Then, for the conditional mutual information
{\mathcal{I}}_{\rho_{\beta}}(A:C|B)={\rm tr}\left[\rho_{\beta}H_{\rho_{\beta}}(A:C|B)\right]
, we obtain

\displaystyle{\rm tr}\left[\rho_{\beta}H_{\rho_{\beta}}(A:C|B)\right]-{\rm tr}\left[\rho_{\beta}H_{\tilde{\rho}_{\beta}}(A:C|B)\right]

\displaystyle=-S\left(\rho_{\beta,AB}|\tilde{\rho}_{\beta,AB}\right)-S\left(\rho_{\beta,BC}|\tilde{\rho}_{\beta,BC}\right)+S\left(\rho_{\beta,ABC}|\tilde{\rho}_{\beta,ABC}\right)+S\left(\rho_{\beta,B}|\tilde{\rho}_{\beta,B}\right).

(S.183)

Therefore, by applying (
S.182
) to the above equation, we have

\displaystyle\left|{\rm tr}\left[\rho_{\beta}H_{\rho_{\beta}}(A:C|B)\right]-{\rm tr}\left[\rho_{\beta}H_{\tilde{\rho}_{\beta}}(A:C|B)\right]\right|\leq 4S\left(\rho_{\beta}|\tilde{\rho}_{\beta}\right)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow{\mathcal{I}}_{\rho_{\beta}}(A:C|B)\leq\left\|H_{\tilde{\rho}_{\beta}}(A:C|B)\right\|+4S\left(\rho_{\beta}|\tilde{\rho}_{\beta}\right).

(S.184)

From the definitions of
\rho_{\beta}
and
\tilde{\rho}_{\beta}
, we can immediately obtain

\displaystyle S\left(\rho_{\beta}|\tilde{\rho}_{\beta}\right)

\displaystyle={\rm tr}\left\{\rho_{\beta}\left[\beta H-\log\left(\tilde{\Phi}_{XY}e^{\beta\left(H_{LX}+H_{L[\ell]^{\rm c}}\right)}\tilde{\Phi}_{XY}^{\dagger}\right)\right]\right\}-\log(Z_{\beta})+\log\left[{\rm tr}\left(\tilde{\Phi}_{XY}e^{\beta\left(H_{LX}+H_{L[\ell]^{\rm c}}\right)}\tilde{\Phi}_{XY}^{\dagger}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\beta H-\log\left(\tilde{\Phi}_{XY}e^{\beta\left(H_{LX}+H_{L[\ell]^{\rm c}}\right)}\tilde{\Phi}_{XY}^{\dagger}\right)\right\|+\delta_{\beta,\ell,L},

(S.185)

where we use the inequality (
S.169
) to obtain

\displaystyle{\rm tr}\left(\tilde{\Phi}_{XY}e^{\beta\left(H_{LX}+H_{L[\ell]^{\rm c}}\right)}\tilde{\Phi}_{XY}^{\dagger}\right)=\left\|\tilde{\Phi}_{XY}e^{\beta\left(H_{LX}+H_{L[\ell]^{\rm c}}\right)}\tilde{\Phi}_{XY}^{\dagger}\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|e^{\beta H}\right\|_{1}+\left\|e^{\beta H}-\tilde{\Phi}_{XY}e^{\beta\left(H_{LX}+H_{L[\ell]^{\rm c}}\right)}\tilde{\Phi}_{XY}^{\dagger}\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq Z_{\beta}(1+\delta_{\beta,\ell,L}).

(S.186)

By applying the inequality (
S.185
) to the inequality (
S.184
), we reach the upper bound of (
S.179
).
This completes the proof of Lemma
14
.
\square

S.III.3
Partial-trace-projection formalism

As has been shown, we cannot utilize the belief propagation technique to derive the entanglement Hamiltonian in high dimensions.
We thus take another route to obtain it.
The statements in this section hold for arbitrary states
\rho
, and are not restricted to thermal states
\rho_{\beta}
.

Let us adopt an ancilla system
L_{a}
and define the maximally entangled states
\ket{{\mathcal{P}}_{L}}
between
L
and
L_{a}
as follows:

\displaystyle\ket{{\mathcal{P}}_{L}}:=\sum_{j=1}^{\mathcal{D}_{L}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{D}_{L}}}\ket{j_{L}}\otimes\ket{j_{L_{a}}},

(S.187)

which gives the partial transpose as

\displaystyle\bra{{\mathcal{P}}_{L}}\rho\otimes\hat{1}_{L_{a}}\ket{{\mathcal{P}}_{L}}=\tilde{{\rm tr}}_{L}(\rho)=\frac{1}{\mathcal{D}_{L}}{\rm tr}_{L}(\rho),

(S.188)

where
\{\ket{j_{L}}\}
and
\{\ket{j_{L_{a}}}\}
are the arbitrary orthonormal bases of the Hilbert spaces on the subsystems
L
and
L_{a}
, respectively.
In the following, we omit the notation of
\hat{1}_{L_{a}}
and simply denote
\rho\otimes\hat{1}_{L_{a}}
by
\rho
.

We thus define

\displaystyle{\mathcal{P}}_{L}:=\ket{{\mathcal{P}}_{L}}\bra{{\mathcal{P}}_{L}},

(S.189)

as the partial-trace projection (PTP) onto the subsystem
L
.
Then, we approximate the PTP by the following exponential form:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{P}}_{L,\tau}:=e^{-\tau{\mathcal{Q}}_{L}},\quad{\mathcal{Q}}_{L}:=1-{\mathcal{P}}_{L}.

(S.190)

By making
\tau\to\infty
, we have
{\mathcal{P}}_{L,\tau}\to{\mathcal{P}}_{L}
with an exponentially small error with
\tau
.
We indeed prove the following lemma:

Lemma 15
.

Let
\rho
be an arbitrary quantum state.
Then, we obtain the norm bound of

\displaystyle\left\|{\mathcal{P}}_{L,\tau}\rho{\mathcal{P}}_{L,\tau}-{\mathcal{P}}_{L}\rho{\mathcal{P}}_{L}\right\|_{1}\leq 2e^{-\tau},

(S.191)

which also yields

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{{\mathcal{P}}_{L,\tau}\rho{\mathcal{P}}_{L,\tau}}{{\rm tr}\left({\mathcal{P}}_{L,\tau}\rho{\mathcal{P}}_{L,\tau}\right)}-\rho_{L^{\rm c}}\otimes{\mathcal{P}}_{L}\right\|_{1}\leq 4\mathcal{D}_{L}e^{-\tau}.

(S.192)

Proof of Lemma
15
.

We start with the inequality of

\displaystyle\left\|{\mathcal{P}}_{L,\tau}\rho{\mathcal{P}}_{L,\tau}-{\mathcal{P}}_{L}\rho{\mathcal{P}}_{L}\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\left\|\left({\mathcal{P}}_{L,\tau}-{\mathcal{P}}_{L}\right)\rho{\mathcal{P}}_{L,\tau}+{\mathcal{P}}_{L}\rho\left({\mathcal{P}}_{L,\tau}-{\mathcal{P}}_{L}\right)\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{\mathcal{P}}_{L,\tau}-{\mathcal{P}}_{L}\right\|\cdot\left\|\rho\right\|_{1}\cdot\left\|{\mathcal{P}}_{L,\tau}\right\|+\left\|{\mathcal{P}}_{L}\right\|\cdot\left\|\rho\right\|_{1}\cdot\left\|{\mathcal{P}}_{L,\tau}-{\mathcal{P}}_{L}\right\|\leq 2\left\|{\mathcal{P}}_{L,\tau}-{\mathcal{P}}_{L}\right\|.

(S.193)

Using
{\mathcal{P}}_{L,\tau}={\mathcal{P}}_{L,\tau}({\mathcal{P}}_{L}+{\mathcal{Q}}_{L})={\mathcal{P}}_{L}+e^{-\tau}{\mathcal{Q}}_{L}
, we reduce the above inequality to the main inequality (
S.191
).
The second inequality (
S.192
) is immediately derived from Eq. (
S.188
) as follows:

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{{\mathcal{P}}_{L,\tau}\rho{\mathcal{P}}_{L,\tau}}{{\rm tr}\left({\mathcal{P}}_{L,\tau}\rho{\mathcal{P}}_{L,\tau}\right)}-\rho_{L^{\rm c}}\otimes{\mathcal{P}}_{L}\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\frac{{\mathcal{P}}_{L,\tau}\rho{\mathcal{P}}_{L,\tau}}{{\rm tr}\left({\mathcal{P}}_{L,\tau}\rho{\mathcal{P}}_{L,\tau}\right)}-\mathcal{D}_{L}{\mathcal{P}}_{L,\tau}\rho{\mathcal{P}}_{L,\tau}\right\|_{1}+\left\|\mathcal{D}_{L}{\mathcal{P}}_{L,\tau}\rho{\mathcal{P}}_{L,\tau}-\rho_{L^{\rm c}}\otimes{\mathcal{P}}_{L}\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{1}{{\rm tr}\left({\mathcal{P}}_{L,\tau}\rho{\mathcal{P}}_{L,\tau}\right)}-\mathcal{D}_{L}\right|\cdot\left\|{\mathcal{P}}_{L,\tau}\rho{\mathcal{P}}_{L,\tau}\right\|_{1}+\left\|\mathcal{D}_{L}{\mathcal{P}}_{L,\tau}\rho{\mathcal{P}}_{L,\tau}-\mathcal{D}_{L}{\mathcal{P}}_{L}\rho{\mathcal{P}}_{L}\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\left|1-\mathcal{D}_{L}\left\|{\mathcal{P}}_{L,\tau}\rho{\mathcal{P}}_{L,\tau}\right\|_{1}\right|+2\mathcal{D}_{L}e^{-\tau}\leq 4\mathcal{D}_{L}e^{-\tau},

(S.194)

where, in the last inequality, we use
\mathcal{D}_{L}\left\|{\mathcal{P}}_{L,\tau}\rho{\mathcal{P}}_{L,\tau}\right\|_{1}\leq\mathcal{D}_{L}\left\|{\mathcal{P}}_{L}\rho{\mathcal{P}}_{L}\right\|_{1}+2\mathcal{D}_{L}e^{-\tau}=1+2\mathcal{D}_{L}e^{-\tau}
from the inequality (
S.191
).
This completes the proof.
\square

[ End of Proof of Lemma
15
]

We then consider the von Neumann entropy for the reduced density matrix
\rho_{L^{\rm c}}
.
From Eq. (
S.188
), we can immediately obtain

\displaystyle S\left(\mathcal{D}_{L}{\mathcal{P}}_{L}\rho{\mathcal{P}}_{L}\right)=S\left(\rho_{L^{\rm c}}\otimes{\mathcal{P}}_{L}\right)=S\left(\rho_{L^{\rm c}}\right)+S\left({\mathcal{P}}_{L}\right)=S\left(\rho_{L^{\rm c}}\right).

(S.195)

Note that
\ket{{\mathcal{P}}_{L}}
is pure state, and hence
S\left({\mathcal{P}}_{L}\right)=0
.
Now, the problem is to estimate the error of the relative entropy of

\displaystyle\left|S\left(\frac{{\mathcal{P}}_{L,\tau}\rho{\mathcal{P}}_{L,\tau}}{{\rm tr}\left({\mathcal{P}}_{L,\tau}\rho{\mathcal{P}}_{L,\tau}\right)}\biggr|\mathcal{D}_{L}{\mathcal{P}}_{L}\rho{\mathcal{P}}_{L}\right)\right|,

(S.196)

which is connected to the conditional mutual information using Eq. (
S.183
).
As in the case of one-dimensional systems, we have to avoid the factor of
\log(\mathcal{D}_{L^{\rm c}})
, which appears from the continuity inequality of the relative entropy
[
162
]
.
As in the following lemma, we can obtain a better continuity bound using the property of
{\mathcal{P}}_{L}
(see Sec.
S.III.3.1
for the proof).

Lemma 16
.

Let
\rho
be an arbitrary density matrix and
\rho_{L^{\rm c},\tau}
be defined as

\displaystyle\rho_{L^{\rm c},\tau}:=\frac{{\mathcal{P}}_{L,\tau}\rho{\mathcal{P}}_{L,\tau}}{{\rm tr}\left({\mathcal{P}}_{L,\tau}\rho{\mathcal{P}}_{L,\tau}\right)}=\frac{e^{-\tau{\mathcal{Q}}_{L}}\rho e^{-\tau{\mathcal{Q}}_{L}}}{{\rm tr}\left(e^{-\tau{\mathcal{Q}}_{L}}\rho e^{-\tau{\mathcal{Q}}_{L}}\right)}.

(S.197)

Then, we obtain the upper bound of

\displaystyle S\left(\rho_{L^{\rm c},\infty}|\rho_{L^{\rm c},\tau}\right)\leq 16\mathcal{\chi}_{\tau,\rho,L^{\rm c}}\mathcal{D}_{L}e^{-\tau},

(S.198)

where
\mathcal{\chi}_{\tau,\rho,L^{\rm c}}
is defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{\chi}_{\tau,\rho,L^{\rm c}}:=\sup_{u_{L}}\left\|\left[\log\left(\rho_{L^{\rm c},\tau}\right),u_{L}\right]\right\|+\int_{\tau}^{\infty}e^{\tau-\tau_{1}}\sup_{u_{L}}\left\|\left[\log\left(\rho_{L^{\rm c},\tau_{1}}\right),u_{L}\right]\right\|d\tau_{1}.

(S.199)

We now combine this lemma with a similar inequality to Eq. (
S.183
), i.e.,

\displaystyle{\rm tr}\left[\rho H_{\rho}(A:C|B)\right]

\displaystyle=-S\left(\rho_{AB,\infty}|\rho_{AB,\tau}\right)-S\left(\rho_{BC,\infty}|\rho_{BC,\tau}\right)+S\left(\rho_{ABC,\infty}|\rho_{ABC,\tau}\right)+S\left(\rho_{B,\infty}|\rho_{B,\tau}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+{\rm tr}\left[\rho H_{\rho,\tau}(A:C|B)\right],

(S.200)

where
H_{\rho,\tau}(A:C|B)
is defined as

\displaystyle H_{\rho,\tau}(A:C|B):=-{\mathcal{P}}_{C}\log(\rho_{AB,\tau}){\mathcal{P}}_{C}-{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\log(\rho_{BC,\tau}){\mathcal{P}}_{A}+\log(\rho_{ABC,\tau})+{\mathcal{P}}_{AC}\log(\rho_{B,\tau}){\mathcal{P}}_{AC}.

(S.201)

Note that from
\rho_{AB,\infty}=\rho_{C^{\rm c},\infty}={\mathcal{P}}_{C}\rho{\mathcal{P}}_{C}
[see Eq. (
S.197
)], we have

\displaystyle S\left(\rho_{AB}\right)=S\left(\rho_{AB,\infty}\right)

\displaystyle=-S\left(\rho_{AB,\infty}|\rho_{AB,\tau}\right)-{\rm tr}\left[\rho_{AB,\infty}\log\left(\rho_{AB,\tau}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=-S\left(\rho_{AB,\infty}|\rho_{AB,\tau}\right)-{\rm tr}\left[\rho{\mathcal{P}}_{C}\log\left(\rho_{AB,\tau}\right){\mathcal{P}}_{C}\right].

(S.202)

From Lemma
16
and the inequality (
S.200
), we can also upper-bound the conditional mutual information, which is given as follows:

Corollary 17
.

For an arbitrary tripartition of
\Lambda=A\sqcup B\sqcup C
, the quantum conditional mutual information
{\mathcal{I}}_{\rho}(A:C|B)
is upper-bounded as follows:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{I}}_{\rho}(A:C|B)\leq 16e^{-\tau}\left(\mathcal{D}_{C}\mathcal{\chi}_{\tau,\rho,AB}+\mathcal{D}_{A}\mathcal{\chi}_{\tau,\rho,BC}+\mathcal{D}_{AC}\mathcal{\chi}_{\tau,\rho,B}\right)+\left\|H_{\rho,\tau}(A:C|B)\right\|.

(S.203)

For the proof, we use
\rho_{L^{\rm c},\tau}=\rho
for
L=\emptyset
(or
L^{\rm c}=\Lambda
), which gives
S\left(\rho_{ABC}|\rho_{ABC,\tau}\right)=0
.

Remark.

To estimate the RHS of the inequality (
S.203
), it is crucial to estimate
\mathcal{\chi}_{\tau,\rho,L^{\rm c}}
in Eq. (
S.199
).
When we consider the quantum Gibbs state,
as will be shown in Corollary
19
[see also Eq. (
S.241
)] and Subtheorem
1
,
the logarithm of
\rho_{\beta,L^{\rm c},\tau}
is expressed in the form of
U_{\tau}(\beta H_{0}+\hat{V}_{\tau})U_{\tau}^{\dagger}
with
V=Q_{L}
.
The theorem implies that the non-locality of the unitary operator
